Approximating fixed size quantum correlations in polynomial time
Julius A. Zeiss 1,∗, Gereon Kossmann 1, Omar Fawzi 2, Mario Berta 1,3

Abstract
. We show that ε \varepsilon -additive approximations of the optimal value of fixed-size two-player free games with fixed-dimensional entanglement assistance can be computed in time poly ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) \mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon) . This stands in contrast to previous analytic approaches, which focused on scaling with the number of questions and answers, but yielded only strict exp ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) \mathrm{exp}(1/\varepsilon) guarantees. Our main result is based on novel Bose-symmetric quantum de Finetti theorems tailored for constrained quantum separability problems. These results give rise to semidefinite programming (SDP) outer hierarchies for approximating the entangled value of such games. By employing representation-theoretic symmetry reduction techniques, we demonstrate that these SDPs can be formulated and solved with computational complexity poly ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) \mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon) , thereby enabling efficient ε \varepsilon -additive approximations. In addition, we introduce a measurement-based rounding scheme that translates the resulting outer bounds into certifiably good inner sequences of entangled strategies. These strategies can, for instance, serve as warm starts for see-saw optimization methods. We believe that our techniques are of independent interest for broader classes of constrained separability problems in quantum information theory.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation

The famous EPR-Paradox
[
EPR35
]
and its resolution by Bell
[
Bel64
]
head started the research into non-local games within the quantum theory community. In this work, we focus exclusively on the standard setting of two-player free non-local games. In these games
G
, two spatially separated cooperative players receive questions
q_{1}\in Q_{1}
,
q_{2}\in Q_{2}
, respectively, chosen independently by a referee according to the probability distributions
\pi_{1}(q_{1}),\,\pi_{2}(q_{2})
. We assume that
\left\lvert{Q_{1}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{Q_{2}}\right\rvert
. The two players are able to agree on an answering strategy beforehand, but once the referee starts to hand out the questions, no further adaptation of said strategy is allowed. As opposed to the classical setting, where a strategy consists of a set of conditioned answers, in the quantum counterpart, conditional measurements are performed on a shared entangled quantum state. To conclude the game, the referee judges the collections of answers
a_{1}\in A_{1},\,a_{2}\in A_{2}
with
\left\lvert{A_{1}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{A_{2}}\right\rvert
with respect to a game-specific rule function
V
. The quantities of interest to us are the players’ maximal winning probability
0\leq w_{Q}(V,\pi)\leq 1
and the strategy attaining this optimal value. In this context, a strategy corresponds to the collection of the parties’ respective sets of complete POVMs and their shared quantum state. Importantly, quantum resources allow for higher winning probabilities in certain games, see, e.g. the CHSH-game
[
Cir80
]
.

Our interest in these games is two-fold. From a physics perspective, as in Bell’s work, the potential gap in the maximal winning probability obtained from games with shared quantum rather than classical resources helps in delineating the boundary between the convex set of quantum correlations from the polytope of classical correlations
[
NPA07
,
NPA08
]
. From a complexity theorist viewpoint, even in the classical variant, approximately determining
w_{C}(V,\pi)
within a constant multiplicative factor is NP-hard (see PCP theorem
[
Aro+92
,
AS92
]
). In addition, computing
w_{Q}(V,\pi)
is, in general, even harder
[
Cle+10
,
IKM09
,
Kem+11
,
BH17
]
. Concretely, its approximation cannot be done in finite time
[
Ji+22
]
. However, in specific games
[
Cle+10
,
Kem+11
]
polynomial time approximations are possible. We follow
[
NTV14
,
Nav+15
,
NV15
,
Jee+21
]
and consider only quantum resources of fixed dimension
T
to be shared among the players and ask how well and at what computational cost can we approximate
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
by classical algorithms?

We will next give a brief overview of our results. The precise definitions of the quantities involved are given in the subsequent sections.

1.2.
Contributions

Leveraging a synthesis of information-theoretic techniques and structural tools from group theory, we resolve several outstanding problems related to the approximation of quantum correlations, as articulated in
[
Jee+21
]
and
[
Ber+21
]
.

Our primary contribution concerns free nonlocal games with
\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert
answers,
\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert
questions, and quantum assistance of fixed dimension
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert
. By exploiting symmetry arguments, we construct two efficient algorithmic variants that approximate the value of such games up to additive error
\epsilon
, via the solution of a semidefinite program (SDP) of size

(1)

\displaystyle\left[\epsilon^{-4}\cdot\mathrm{poly}\left(|A|,|Q|,|T|\right)\right]^{\left(|A||Q||T|\right)^{2}}.\,

In particular, for constant-sized games, this yields a
\text{poly}\left({\epsilon^{-1}}\right)
scaling. The result is a consequence of several key ideas, which we detail below:

(A)

We mitigate the dependence on the quantum dimension
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert
by leveraging methods from quantum steering (see also
[
Ara22
]
), thereby reformulating the problem of determining the maximum winning probability of a two-player free non-local game with fixed quantum assistance as an optimization problem of the constrained bipartite separability variety (see
[
Ber+21
]
). A permutation-symmetry-based semidefinite programming (SDP) hierarchy then provides converging upper bounds on the value of the game. The rate of convergence is certified by a new constrained de Finetti representation theorem, which extends
[
Ber+21
, Theorem 2.3]
to accommodate a broader class of constraints than those treated in
[
Ber+21
,
Jee+21
]
, thereby addressing the constrained structure of the underlying separability problem. Namely, we show that for
A=A_{L}\otimes A_{R}
,
B=B_{L}\otimes B_{R}
, and a quantum state
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
symmetric w.r.t.
A
satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}B_{1}^{n}},&\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}},\\
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{n}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{B_{1}^{n-1}\left({B_{R}}\right)_{n}},&\Xi_{B_{n}\rightarrow B_{n}}\left({\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\rho_{B_{1}^{n}},\\
\end{array}

for linear maps
\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}
,
\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}
,
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{n}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}
,
\Xi_{B_{n}\rightarrow B_{n}}
and constant operators
W_{C_{A_{L}}}
,
K_{B_{L}}
, we have

(4)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{AB}-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\rho_{A}^{x}\otimes\rho_{B}^{x}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|B|}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\sqrt{\ln{|A|}}\right),\,

where
\forall x\in\mathcal{X}:

(5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{A}^{x}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}}^{x},&\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{A}^{x}}\right)=\rho_{A}^{x},\\
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\rho_{B}^{x}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{B}^{x},&\Xi_{B\rightarrow B}\left({\rho_{B}^{x}}\right)=\rho_{B}^{x}\,.\end{array}\end{split}

Compared to previous work (see
Sec. 1.4
), this already features improved error bounds w.r.t. the quantum assistance available to the two players.

(B)

Now, for unconstrained optimizations over separable states, so-called Bose-symmetric de Finetti representation theorems based on symmetric subspace methods are commonly used in symmetry-based approaches. While symmetric states may be supported on the entire Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
, Bose-symmetric states are characterized by having both their support and range confined strictly to the symmetric subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
, which in turn, is of polynomial dimension w.r.t.
n
. While it has previously been unclear how to incorporate linear constraints for Bose-symmetric considerations, we achieve exactly that and derive novel Bose-symmetric de Finetti representation theorems subject to a variety of linear constraints. Namely, we show that for quantum states
\rho_{A(B\bar{B})_{1}^{n}}
Bose-symmetric on
\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}
w.r.t.
A
and

(6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}},&\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}},\\
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{n}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\circ\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{B}_{n}}\left({\rho_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n-1}\left({B_{R}}\right)_{n}},&\Xi_{B_{n}\rightarrow B_{n}}\circ\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{B}_{n}}\left({\rho_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\rho_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n-1}B_{n}},\\
\end{array}\end{split}

we have

(7)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{AB\bar{B}}-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\rho_{A}^{x}\otimes\rho_{B\bar{B}}^{x}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|B\bar{B}|}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\sqrt{\ln{|A|}}\right),\,

where
\forall x\in\mathcal{X}:

(8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{A}^{x}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}}^{x},&\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{A}^{x}}\right)=\rho_{A}^{x}\\
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\circ\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{B}}\left[{\rho_{B\bar{B}}^{x}}\right]=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{B_{R}}^{x},&\Xi_{B\rightarrow B}\circ\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{B}}\left[{\rho_{B}^{x}}\right]=\rho_{B}^{x}\,.\end{array}\end{split}

Together with a tailor-made purification scheme based on the pretty-good purification, the constrained Bose-symmetric de Finetti theorem enables the formulation of a novel Bose-symmetric SDP hierarchy that yields converging outer approximations for any constrained separability problem. We refer to
Sec. 5
for precise definitions and full details.

(C)

Achieving an overall polynomial runtime requires not only that the SDP be of polynomial size, but also that it can be constructed in polynomial time. We establish this by leveraging Bose-symmetric methods to construct the SDP directly in a symmetry-adapted basis. Based on these methods, the resulting computational complexity of approximating the value of the game up to an additive
\epsilon
-error relies on an isomorphism to a full matrix algebra over
\mathbb{C}
of dimension
\text{poly}\left({\epsilon^{-1}}\right)
. Crucially, we show that this isomorphism—and the corresponding SDP—can be computed in time polynomial in
\epsilon^{-1}
, thereby yielding the complexity bound stated in
Eq. 1
.

(D)

Alternatively, starting from the permutation-symmetry-based methods and using the conceptually distinct mathematical framework for standard symmetry reduction from e.g.
[
CTV24
,
LPS16
,
FST22
,
Gij09
]
, we develop a similar polynomial-time algorithm based on an isomorphism into blocks of full matrix algebras over
\mathbb{C}
of size
\text{poly}\left({\epsilon^{-1}}\right)
. In regimes where the underlying system dimensions are large, this algorithm outperforms the Bose-symmetric candidate in terms of practical computational efficiency. We refer to
Sec. 1.4
for previous work in this direction.

(E)

Finally, we leverage the outer approximations from aforementioned SDP hierarchies to develop an effective rounding procedure with rigorous convergence guarantees (cf. our concurrent work
[
Koß+25
]
). This produces feasible strategies in terms of shared entangled states with corresponding conditional measurements, which provide lower bounds on the game’s value.

Although we developed and applied techniques to address a game-theoretic instance of optimization over the constrained separability set, our methods hold promise for future applications to other problems within this class. For example, in the context of approximate quantum error correction
[
Ber+21
]
, an alternative symmetry reduction method based on the constrained Bose-symmetry could be explored as an alternative over the standard SDP symmetry reduction approach in
[
CTV24
]
(cf. our concurrent work
[
Koß+25
]
).

1.3.
Methods

Our approach integrates concepts from quantum information theory to derive the key results, utilizing ideas from quantum steering, symmetric subspace methods, Schur-Weyl duality, Weyl’s tensorial construction, matrix block-decomposition, and ring theory. Below, we outline the mathematical framework and procedural steps employed in our analysis.

We reformulate non-local games as constrained bipartite separability problems and, using an adapted DPS hierarchy, construct a hierarchy of outer approximations with convergence rates quantified by an information-theoretic de Finetti representation theorem adapted to our setting. A tailored SDP rounding procedure, supported by convergence guarantees derived from a ’reverse’ application of the aforementioned de Finetti theorem, is employed to obtain suitable lower bounds and feasible strategies. To address the computational complexity of determining the value of fixed size two-player free non-local games with bounded quantum assistance, we leverage the inherent symmetry of states in DPS hierarchies. The algorithmic procedure mentioned in
(C)
extends well-known symmetric subspace methods to accommodate additional linear constraints. While existing techniques rely on representing separable states as a convex mixture of pure product states, the constrained separable states set does not admit such a representation. We address these challenges using a local purification scheme in conjunction with a constrained Bose-symmetric de Finetti representation theorem as sketched in
Eq. 7
–
Eq. 8
. The isomorphism into a polynomial sized SDP is then based on a weight space decomposition of the symmetric subspace. The procedure mentioned in
(D)
implements an SDP symmetry reduction in the spirit of e.g.
[
CTV24
,
LPS16
,
FST22
,
Gij09
,
Val09
]
by decomposing the support space under the action of the exchange symmetry group.

The remainder of this manuscript is structured as follows. We first discuss previous related work in
Sec. 1.4
. Then, we present in
Sec. 2
an overview of our results in the context of the quantum information literature and techniques used to derive them. The section concludes with an outlook on potential future applications and open problems. Thereafter, we present the corresponding proofs in detail. This includes the following steps: In
Sec. 3
, we reformulate fixed-size non-local games as a bipartite constrained separability problem. Then, in
Sec. 4
, we construct a sequence of inner approximations to these games.
Sec. 5
introduces a constrained Bose-symmetric hierarchy, along with a de Finetti theorem, which serves to approximate the values of the games from above. The efficient computation of this hierarchy is demonstrated in
Sec. 6
, leading to our main contribution: a polynomial-time algorithm for approximating the value of fixed-size free two-player non-local games. Finally, we conclude with an SDP symmetry reduction in
Sec. 7
, providing an alternative polynomial-time algorithm. Any further material, including supplementary derivations and extended analyses, are provided in
App. A
–
.

1.4.
Previous work

Important for this work,
[
Jee+21
]
provided insights to the question of how well
w_{Q(T)}(G)
can be approximated by formulating an equivalent constrained tripartite separability problem. The authors formulate a corresponding SDP relaxation — inspired from but also crucially different to the plain DPS-hierarchy (cf. the discussion in our concurrent work
[
Koß+25
]
)
[
DPS05
,
DPS04
,
NOP09a
,
NOP09
]
— in terms of strategies with a certain exchange symmetry rather than separability, obtaining a hierarchy of improving approximates converging to the true optimal value. The proof of convergence employed a de Finetti representation theorem based on information-theoretic arguments (see e.g.
[
CFS02
,
KR05
,
Chr+07
,
KM09
,
RC09
,
Chi11
,
BH17
,
Ber+21
,
Har13
,
LGG23
,
GNW21
]
for variants of de Finetti theorems in quantum physics), bounding the trace distance between these symmetric approximate strategies subject to linear constraints and actually feasible strategies.
[
Jee+21
]
provide an SDP of size

(9)

\displaystyle\exp\left[{\epsilon^{-2}\cdot\mathcal{O}\left({\text{poly}\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert\cdot\log\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert\left({\log\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert+\log\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert}\right)}\right)}\right]

— quasi-polynomial in
A
, polynomial in
Q
, but exponential in
\epsilon
— to estimate
w_{Q(T)}(G)
up to an additive
\epsilon
-error. Their result differs from those of
[
Nav+15
,
NV15
]
, which focused more on practical implementations than on rigorous analytical guarantees. Nevertheless, the
\epsilon
-dependence introduced by symmetry constraints poses significant challenges for practical applications where one is typically not interested in the scaling in
A
and
Q
, but rather has fixed size
A
and
Q
(e.g.  equal to two) and would then like to approximate the optimal game value up to some pre-fixed approximation
\epsilon>0
.

This motivates us to focus our study on these symmetries in order to effectively reduce their computational cost. Similarly,
[
DPS04
,
LPS16
,
IR22
,
DPS07
,
Ros18
,
Gij09
,
Val09
]
exploited the inherent symmetry in various optimization problems to formulate classical algorithms providing solutions in polynomial time. However, the symmetric objects in these works need not adhere to any linear constraints. A subtlety rendering any adaptation to our scenario ineffective. Recently,
[
CTV24
]
provided a novel symmetry-reduced hierarchy approximating the quantum channel fidelity in a coding task. To our knowledge, this constitutes the first time where such a symmetry reduction in the presence of additional linear constraints was carried out. Lastly, although Bose-symmetry-based reductions (e.g.
[
Chr+07
,
NOP09a
]
) are available, they are not applicable to the constrained case.

2.
Overview of results

2.1.
Two-player non-local games

This work focuses on the standard setting of two-player (provers) one-round free non-local games. In these games,
G
, two spatially separated cooperative players, Alice and Bob, are up against a third party, the referee (verifier). The referee chooses questions
q_{1},\,q_{2}
with probability
\pi_{1}(q_{1}),\,\pi_{2}(q_{2})
from given finite alphabets
Q_{1},\,Q_{2}
and distributes them to Alice and Bob, respectively. We assume the description of
\pi
is polynomial in size of
Q_{i}

[
Kem+11
]
. Conditioned on the questions the players receive, they return answers
a_{1},\,a_{2}
from finite alphabets
A_{1},\,A_{2}
, respectively. To systematically judge the success of the players in answering the referees’ questions, the
rule function

(10)

\displaystyle V:A_{1}\times A_{2}\times Q_{1}\times Q_{2}\rightarrow\{0,1\}

takes on
V(a_{1},a_{2},q_{1},q_{2})=1
whenever the players win the game and
0
otherwise. While the players cannot communicate once the referee has distributed the questions, they can strategize beforehand. We follow
[
NTV14
,
Nav+15
,
NV15
,
Jee+21
]
and focus on those games where the players only have access to bounded quantum assistance. Both parties agree on an arbitrary shared bipartite quantum state
\rho_{T\hat{T}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H}_{T}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\hat{T}})
1
1

1

\mathcal{H}_{\hat{T}}
is an isomorphic copy of
\mathcal{H}_{T}
, thus dim
{}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{H}_{T})=\text{dim}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{H}_{\hat{T}})=\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert
.
beforehand. Upon receiving questions
q_{1},\,q_{2}
they perform measurements from a complete set of local POVMs
\{E_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\}_{a_{1}}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{T})
and
\{D_{\hat{T}}(a_{2}|q_{2})\}_{a_{2}}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\hat{T}})
return the answers to the referee. The completeness condition on Alice’s and Bob’s respective sets of POVMs to hold for each tuple of questions
(q_{1},\,q_{2})
yields them to be non-signaling, i.e. neither Alice nor Bob can communicate their respective question to the other party via measurements. Thus, the value of the game
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
is determined by solving the following optimization problem:

(11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)=&\max_{(\rho,\,E,\,D)}\hskip 14.22636pt\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\pi_{2}(q_{2})\sum_{a_{1},a_{2}}V(a_{1},a_{2},q_{1},q_{2})\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(E_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\otimes D_{\hat{T}}(a_{2}|q_{2})\right)\rho_{T\hat{T}}\right]\\
\\
\text{s.t.}\quad&\rho_{T\hat{T}}\succcurlyeq 0,\,\quad\mathrm{tr}\left[\rho_{T\hat{T}}\right]=1,\quad\text{state condition,}\\
&E_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\succcurlyeq 0\quad\forall a_{1},q_{1},\,\hskip 28.45274pt\sum_{a_{1}}E_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})=\mathcal{I}_{T}\quad\forall q_{1},\quad\text{POVMs,}\\
&D_{\hat{T}}(a_{2}|q_{2})\succcurlyeq 0\quad\forall a_{2},q_{2},\,\hskip 28.45274pt\sum_{a_{2}}D_{\hat{T}}(a_{2}|q_{2})=\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}\quad\forall q_{2},\quad\text{POVMs.}\end{split}

In this paper, we derive new outer and inner bounds on
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
, along with algorithms that, for fixed
\epsilon
, compute
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
with an additive
\epsilon
-error in polynomial time and space.

Notation

Henceforth, let
\mathcal{H}
be a finite-dimensional Hilbert space of dimension
\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert
, i.e.
\mathcal{H}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}
. We assume
\left\lvert{A_{1}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{A_{2}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert
and
\left\lvert{Q_{1}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{Q_{2}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert
and abbreviate
\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{AQT}\right\rvert
. We often abbreviate
\mathcal{H}_{T}
to
T
of dimension
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert
and denote the identity by
\mathcal{I}_{T}
. The binary logarithm is denoted by
\log
and the natural logarithm by
\ln
. By poly
(k)
, we denote a scaling that is at most polynomial in
k\in\mathbb{R}
.

2.2.
Non-local games as constrained separability problems

In our first contribution, we avoid the complexities arising from the tripartite formulation in
[
Jee+21
]
, such as e.g. multipartite entanglement
[
Nav+20
,
WGE17
,
Sza15
,
Hor+09
]
. We leverage techniques from quantum steering to reformulate the problem of determining the value of a non-local game with bounded quantum assistance into a linear optimization over bipartite separable states subject to additional linear constraints. This reformulation motivates us to introduce a new variant of the constrained separability problem (cSEP) (see also
[
Ohs+24
]
), formulated as follows

(12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{array}[]{ccc}&cSEP(G):=\displaystyle\max_{\rho_{AB}\in\mathcal{B}(AB)}\mathrm{tr}[G_{AB}(\rho_{AB})]&\\
&&\\
\text{s.t. }&\rho_{AB}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\rho^{x}_{A}\otimes\rho^{x}_{B}&\text{s.th. }\forall x\in\mathcal{X}:\\
&&\\
&\rho^{x}_{A}\succeq 0,\,\rho^{x}_{B}\succeq 0,&\mathrm{tr}\left[\rho^{x}_{A}\right]=1,\,\mathrm{tr}\left[\rho^{x}_{B}\right]=1,\\
&&\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\text{Local constraints }\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{X}:\hfil\lx@intercol&\\
&&\\
&\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho^{x}_{A}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho^{x}_{A_{R}},&\Upsilon_{B_{L}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\rho^{x}_{B}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho^{x}_{B_{R}},\\
&&\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\text{Fixed point constraints }\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{X}:\hfil\lx@intercol&\\
&&\\
&\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho^{x}_{A}}\right)=\rho^{x}_{A},&\Xi_{B\rightarrow B}\left({\rho^{x}_{B}}\right)=\rho^{x}_{B},\\
\end{array}\end{split}

where
\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}},\Upsilon_{B_{L}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}
are linear maps locally imposing a constraint on Alice’s and Bob’s marginal state via the constant operators
W_{C_{A_{L}}},\,K_{C_{B_{L}}}
and linear maps
\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}
,
\Xi_{B\rightarrow B}
enforcing fixed point constraints on those marginal states. Problems of this form represent strict generalizations of cSEP problems studied in
[
Ber+21
,
Jee+21
,
Ohs+24
]
.

To avoid the computational hardness results associated with the set of separable states (see, e.g.,
[
Gur03
,
Ioa07
,
Gha09
,
GLS93
,
SW12
,
Faw21
]
), we construct an SDP hierarchy by adapting the DPS hierarchy
[
DPS04
]
to the constrained setting, in the spirit of
[
Ber+21
,
Jee+21
]
, combined with a novel information-theoretic finite de Finetti representation theorem adapted to this broader class of cSEP problems. This approach allows us to establish the following algorithmic approximation result.

Lemma 2.1
.

The value of a two-player free non-local game with
\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert
answers
\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert
questions and quantum assistance of size
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert
can be approximated by an additive
\epsilon
-error by solving an SDP of size

(13)

\displaystyle\exp\left[{\frac{\mathcal{O}\left({\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert^{6}\log^{2}\left\lvert{AQT}\right\rvert}\right)}{\epsilon^{2}}}\right].\,

Thus, the maximal winning probability with bounded quantum assistance can be approximated within an additive error
\epsilon>0
in
quasi-polynomial time
2
2

2

Following, e.g.
[
Jee+21
,
Ioa07
,
FFF25
]
, and disregarding lower-order contributions from iterative algorithms, we assume that a semidefinite program with
m
variables and matrix size
k
has time complexity
\mathcal{O}\left({m^{2}k^{2}}\right)
.
with respect to the cardinality of the answer and question sets. In comparison to
[
Jee+21
]
scaling result

(14)

\displaystyle\exp\left[{\epsilon^{-2}\cdot\mathcal{O}\left({\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert^{12}\log\left\lvert{AT}\right\rvert\left({\log\left\lvert{AT}\right\rvert+\log\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert}\right)}\right)}\right],\,

we obtain improved bounds w.r.t. the quantum dimension
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert
at the cost of a worse scaling in terms of
\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert
. Thus, our first contribution joins a sequence of works improving the upper bound on
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
for two-player free non-local games in a relevant regime. In this work, our primary contribution is the use of symmetry-based arguments to refine and simplify the approximation algorithm w.r.t.
\epsilon
.

2.3.
Approximating constrained separability problems via Bose-symmetry

In the fixed size game setting, we derive the following efficiency of approximation result.

Lemma 2.2
.

For a fixed size game, the value of
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
can be approximated up to an additive
\epsilon
-error by solving an SDP, denoted
SDP_{n}^{Bose}(V,\pi)
), with
\text{poly}\left({\epsilon^{-1}}\right)
many variables.

The reduction in complexity is fundamentally based on a symmetry argument intrinsic to
n
-extendable states, which are central to the DPS hierarchy. We lift the concept of Bose-symmetric states
[
HM76
,
Wer89
,
Chr+07
]
, i.e. those with support and range on the symmetric subspace
[
Har13
]
, to optimization problems where additional linear constraints are present. On a technical level, these constraints preclude a straightforward adaptation of the techniques from
[
DPS04
,
Ioa07
,
NOP09a
]
, which rely on expressing separable states as convex mixtures of pure product states. Concretely, in the game setting, the DPS hierarchy is working to approximate a convex mixture of POVM elements in a tensor product relation with state assemblages. While a further decomposition into pure objects is possible, these are in general no longer POVM elements or state assemblages. Through a tailored local purification, we construct an embedding of permutation-invariant states subject to linear constraints into the symmetric subspace of a higher-dimensional space, intersected with the corresponding constraints. We demonstrate that for any constrained separability problem, there exists a converging hierarchy of
n
-Bose-symmetric states that approximates the optimal value from above. This convergence is rigorously certified through a corresponding finite and constrained Bose-symmetric de Finetti representation theorem.

Lemma 2.3
.

For
cSEP(G)
as in
Eq. 12
, there exists a convergent SDP hierarchy
SDP_{n}^{Bose}(G)
of outer approximations over Bose-symmetric states, such that:

(15)

\displaystyle 0\leq SDP_{n}^{Bose}(G)-cSEP(G)\leq\mathcal{O}\left({\text{poly}\left({\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert}\right)\sqrt{\frac{\ln\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert}{n}}}\right).\,

We then show that the optimization in
SDP_{n}^{Bose}(G)
can be restricted to the space of
n
and
n-1
Bose-symmetric operators. Although these spaces have polynomial dimension in
n
, the computational cost of determining an explicit representation of
SDP_{n}^{Bose}(G)
in terms of objects of polynomial size in
n
cannot be neglected. In implementations
[
DPS04
,
Ioa07
,
NOP09a
]
, this bottleneck is not directly discussed as the complexity of the SDP is primarily dictated by the number of free variables. While this line of argument is perfectly viable for most practical purposes, it lacks the mathematical rigor necessary to analyze the computational complexity of algorithmic procedures to determine the value of a non-local game. Following e.g.
[
Gij09
,
Pol20
,
CTV24
]
, and as detailed in
Sec. 2.5
, we address this gap by providing a rigorous analysis of the computational costs associated with transforming
SDP_{n}^{Bose}(G)
into a representation involving only objects of size polynomial in
n
. To the best of our knowledge, this work constitutes the first use of the symmetric subspace to approximate constrained separability problems, thereby resolving an open question raised in
[
Jee+21
,
Ber+21
]
, while also presenting the first detailed complexity analysis of methods based on symmetric subspaces.

Crucially, the results of the previous section enable the approximation of
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
via the proposed Bose-symmetric hierarchy, yielding an approximation algorithm with runtime polynomial in the inverse of the approximation error.

2.4.
Convergent sequence of inner bounds to constrained separability problems

While there exists a variety of outer approximations to relevant optimization problems obtained from suitable relaxations of the corresponding constraints, obtaining good inner approximations is often not as straightforward. Due to the non-convexity of the optimization landscape, see-saw methods may become trapped in local minima, and the approaches in
[
NOP09
,
Ohs+24a
]
are not compatible with the additional linear constraints imposed in our setting. Using an effective rounding procedure based on information-theoretic tools, we construct a sequence of inner approximations for constrained separability problems, accompanied by rigorous analytical convergence guarantees, which provide lower bounds on
cSEP(G)
. With respect to non-local games, the rounding procedure is turning a solution of an SDP relaxation to a solution of the actual game.

Theorem 2.4
.

There exists a rounding procedure that converts an
\epsilon
-good approximation obtained from the SDP in
Lem. 2.1
or
SDP_{n}^{Bose}(G)
to
Lem. 2.3
of
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
from above into an
\epsilon
-good approximation from below corresponding to a feasible strategy of Alice’s and Bob’s sets of complete POVMs and a shared quantum state of fixed dimension.

We give an achievability result by explicitly conducting the rounding procedure. The obtained inner points not only serve as warm starts for see-saw procedures but also, thanks to the rigorous convergence guarantees, enable us to establish a complexity result for an algorithmic procedure that determines
w_{Q(T)}
with an additive
\epsilon
-error from both above and below.

2.5.
Polynomial time algorithm approximating quantum non-local games via symmetry reductions

We provide an efficient classical transformation realizing
Lem. 2.2
by representing
SDP_{n}^{Bose}(G)
in terms of the Schur basis, where objects are of size polynomial in
n
. Specifically, we develop a polynomial-time algorithm for approximating the value of a two-player non-local free game with bounded quantum assistance, leveraging Bose-symmetry arguments.

Theorem 2.5
.

For a two-player free non-local game with
\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert
answers,
\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert
questions and quantum assistance of size
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert
, there exists a poly
\left({\epsilon^{-1}}\right)
costly transformation (see
Sec. 5
and
Sec. 6
for further details) to an SDP with

(16)

\displaystyle\left({\epsilon^{-4}\cdot\mathrm{poly}\left({\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert,\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert,\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right)}\right)^{\left({\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right)^{2}},\,

many variables, which approximates the value of the game up to an additive
\epsilon
-error.

In particular, for fixed sized games such as the CHSH-game, we can approximate the optimal value of the game in
\text{poly}\left({\epsilon^{-1}}\right)
.

At the core of an efficient (in
n
) representation of
SDP_{n}^{Bose}(G)
lies the key observation that permutation symmetry—in particular, Bose symmetry—induces significant redundancy in representations that are not adapted to the underlying symmetry. Specifically, a Bose-symmetric operator expressed in the canonical basis results in a matrix of exponential size in
n
, yet contains only polynomially many distinct entries. Consequently, identifying a basis that eliminates these redundancies enables a compact, symmetry-adapted, and efficient representation of
SDP_{n}^{Bose}(G)
denoted by
\Psi\left({SDP_{n}^{Bose}(G)}\right)
. It is crucial to avoid first constructing
SDP_{n}^{Bose}
, followed by a basis transformation to obtain
\Psi\left({SDP_{n}^{Bose}}\right)
, as this would require handling exponentially large objects in
n
. Instead, we show how
\Psi\left({SDP_{n}^{Bose}}\right)
can be constructed directly, in a manner that is efficient with respect to
n
.

For the mathematically inclined reader, we will briefly elaborate the mathematical concepts that motivate
Thm. 2.5
. While the symmetric subspace has been the subject of extensive study (see, e.g.
[
Har13
,
Ren06
,
Chr06
]
), comparatively less attention has been given to the space of Bose-symmetric operators and its elements. The representation theory of the symmetric group
S_{n}
and the general linear group
GL(\mathcal{H})
together with Schur-Weyl duality
[
FH04
,
Ste16
,
Chr06
]
offer a robust mathematical framework for advancing our understanding of the space of Bose-symmetric operators. Firstly, we show that the space of these operators is isomorphic to a full matrix algebra over
\mathbb{C}
of polynomial size in
n
. This result leverages the
S_{n}\times\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-bimodule structure of the symmetric subspace
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
, as established through Schur-Weyl duality. As a
S_{n}\times\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-bimodule
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
decomposes into a simple Specht module and a simple Weyl module. However, as an
S_{n}
-module it decomposes into a direct sum of isomorphic trivial Specht modules. The multiplicity of this isotypic block is given by the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space dimension of the corresponding Weyl module in the bimodule decomposition

(17)

\displaystyle m_{(n)}=\binom{n+\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert-1}{n}.\,

As a
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-module,
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
is isomorphic to a highest weight module given by the Weyl module in the bimodule decomposition. The multiplicity is one, as this module pairs with the trivial Specht module in
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
, which has
\mathbb{C}
-vector space dimension one. Since the span of the image of
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
) in
\text{End}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
is
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
, i.e. the space of
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-invariant endomorphisms on the symmetric subspace, we can argue on Bose-symmetric operators via highest-weight theory, i.e. the representation theory of
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
. Furthermore, the double-centralizer theorem and Maschke’s theorem certify that the image of the complex group algebra
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
in
\text{End}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
is
\text{End}_{\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
and the centralizer of
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
. As
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
is simple as a
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-module, by Schur’s lemma, the
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-commutant in
\text{End}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
is a division ring and therefore
\mathbb{C}\cdot\text{Id}|_{\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}
, i.e.
\text{End}_{\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)\simeq\mathbb{C}
. As the corresponding centralizer algebra
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
is just
\text{End}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
and thus

(18)

\displaystyle\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)\simeq\mathbb{C}^{m_{(n)}\times m_{(n)}}.\,

To concretely realize the isomorphism, we consider a weight space decomposition of the highest weight module
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
. The weight spaces are isomorphic as
S_{n}
-modules. We utilize the correspondence with semistandard Young labelings of the single-row Young shape containing
n
boxes. This weight structure is subsequently employed to construct a canonical basis for the space of Bose-symmetric operators. Given that these weight spaces are spanned by
S_{n}
-orbit averages, the isomorphism is realized as a change of basis from a representation in terms of the canonical basis to
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
to a basis of weight vectors spanning simple
S_{n}
-modules. Based on these insights, for any Weyl-Specht module paring we provide an explicit bijection preserving semi-definiteness realizing a corresponding isomorphism to
Eq. 18
for any such
S_{n}\times\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-bimodule in
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
. To complete the proof, we demonstrate that this change of basis transformation can be performed efficiently with respect to
n
whenever we restrict the constraint maps to be partial trace maps. To achieve this, we avoid explicitly constructing the canonical basis for the space of Bose-symmetric operators, as these objects grow exponentially in size with
n
. Using symmetry arguments, we demonstrate that it is sufficient to restrict attention to a polynomial number of entries in
n
for any Bose-symmetric operator. We show that the transformation in
Thm. 2.5
can be efficiently computed using only these entries. Thus, we provide a polynomial time algorithm to determine the value of a two-player non-local game up to an additive error.

As an alternative procedure, following e.g.
[
CTV24
,
LPS16
,
FST22
,
Gij09
,
Val09
]
, we extend the notions of the previous section to all of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
. Instead of limiting the discussion to the symmetric subspace, corresponding to Specht and Weyl modules associated with the partition of
n
into a single row, we now extend our analysis to the full decomposition of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
into Weyl-modules
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
and Specht modules
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
indexed in partitions of
n
of height at most
d_{\mathcal{H}}
, i.e.
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
decomposes into
\bigoplus_{\lambda\vdash_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}n}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}}\right)^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}\right)}

which yields a decomposition of
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)
into
\bigoplus_{\lambda\vdash_{\,d_{\mathcal{H}}}n}\mathbb{C}^{m_{\lambda}\times m_{\lambda}}
, where
m_{\lambda}\propto\text{poly}(n)
for all
\lambda
and the direct sum contains polynomial in
n
many terms. As a result, we avoid the purification scheme and obtain a polynomial time algorithms for non-local games based on a more complex block-decomposition. Specifically, we establish the following theorem.

Theorem 2.6
.

For a two-player free non-local game with
\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert
answers
\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert
questions and quantum assistance of size
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert
, there exists a poly
\left({\epsilon^{-1}}\right)
costly transformation (see
Sec. 7
for further details) to an SDP with

(19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left({\left\lvert{AQT}\right\rvert^{2}\left({\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert^{6}\frac{\log\left\lvert{AQT}\right\rvert}{\epsilon^{2}}}\right)^{\left\lvert{AQT}\right\rvert^{2}}}\right)

many variables and

(20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left({\left({\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert^{6}\frac{\log\left\lvert{AQT}\right\rvert}{\epsilon^{2}}}\right)^{\left\lvert{AQT}\right\rvert}}\right)

many positive semidefinite constraints and involving matrices of at most size

(21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left({\left\lvert{AQT}\right\rvert^{4}\left({\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert^{6}\frac{\log\left\lvert{AQT}\right\rvert}{\epsilon^{2}}}\right)^{\left({\left\lvert{AQT}\right\rvert\left({\left\lvert{AQT}\right\rvert-1}\right)}\right)}}\right),\,

that approximates the value of the game up to an additive
\epsilon
-error.

This represents a polynomial-time algorithm with respect to
\epsilon^{-1}
for determining the value of a fixed non-local free game up to an additive
\epsilon
-error, thereby offering an alternative efficient procedure. In contrast to the Bose-symmetry-based algorithm, the symmetry variant does not require a purification step and therefore outperforms the former when the local dimensions – i.e.,
\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert,\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert
and
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert
– are large.

For the readers convenience, we provide an overview of the optimization problems considered in this work.

Non-local games

Figure 1.
Overview of optimization problems arising from non-local games and their semidefinite programming (SDP) relaxations. We formulate the value
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
as a constrained quantum separability problem. Two outer approximation hierarchies are introduced:
SDP_{n}(V,\pi)
, based on optimizations over symmetric (i.e.,
n
-extendable) states, and
SDP_{n}^{Bose}(V,\pi)
, based on Bose-symmetric states. Convergence of both hierarchies to
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
is guaranteed by corresponding constrained de Finetti theorems. Each hierarchy admits an efficient reduction to a symmetry-adapted form. Arrows indicate the direction of logical implication between objects.

2.6.
Outlook

Future applications of the techniques developed in this work extend to other problems in quantum information theory that can be formulated as instances of the constrained separability problem. In the context of approximate quantum error correction
[
Ber+21
]
, the novel constrained Bose-symmetric hierarchy introduced here may be compared to existing symmetry reduction approaches, such as those proposed in
[
CTV24
]
. Conversely, following our concurrent work
[
Koß+25
]
, we ask whether an improved complexity for the approximation algorithm can be achieved by combining specific game symmetries—arising from the structure of the rule function—with the exchange symmetry inherent to
n
-extendable states. Furthermore, systematically benchmarking competing numerical methods for constrained separability problems against our Bose-symmetric hierarchy in various concrete applications will offer valuable insights into their relative performance.

In quantum information theory, other variants of Schur-Weyl duality arise. For instance, in
[
GNW21
]
, the authors established a duality between the Clifford group and the stochastic orthogonal group, along with a corresponding de Finetti theorem that enables a converging SDP hierarchy
[
Bel24
]
, akin in spirit to those considered in this work. While these methods are particularly useful in the study of stabilizer codes, the associated procedures remain computationally expensive. Although some symmetry reduction techniques exist
[
HG19
]
, it would be of interest to identify an analogous concept to Bose-symmetry, along with a corresponding hierarchy, that leads to improved complexity results.

3.
Non-local games as constrained separability problems

In this section, we reformulate free two-player non-local games as constrained bipartite separability problems. See
App. A
for an overview of problems of that form. The structure of this section is as follows: First, we demonstrate that the problem of determining the value of a non-local game with bounded quantum assistance can be reformulated as a bipartite constrained separability problem. Next, similar to
[
Ber+21
]
, we outline the construction of a corresponding semidefinite programming (SDP) hierarchy of outer approximations and establish its convergence, leveraging a new bipartite de Finetti theorem, which we state in
App. A
.

3.1.
Notation

Henceforth, we denote the
n
-fold tensor product of a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
by
\mathcal{H}^{n}
, or equivalently,
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{n}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{j}:=\mathcal{H}_{i}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{j}
with
\mathcal{H}_{i}\simeq\mathcal{H}_{j}
for any
i,j\in\left[{n}\right]
. The space of bounded linear operators with support on
\mathcal{H}
is denoted as
\mathcal{B}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)=\text{End}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
and, correspondingly,
\mathcal{S}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
denotes state space. By
\rho\in\mathcal{S}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
we always denote a quantum state, i.e. a normalized (
\mathrm{tr}\left[{\rho}\right]=1
) positive semidefinite (
\rho\succcurlyeq 0
) operator. We denote the adjoint operation by
(\cdot)^{*}
. For any operator
Z_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{2}}\in\mathcal{B}\left({\mathcal{H}_{1}^{2}}\right)
,
Z_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}:=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}\left({Z_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{2}}}\right)
with
partial trace
\mathrm{tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}\left({\cdot}\right)=\sum_{i\in\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}_{2}}\right\rvert}\right]}\left({\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\otimes\bra{i}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}}\right)\left({\cdot}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\otimes\ket{i}_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}}\right)
. When clear from context, system indices are omitted, and identity operations on unaffected subsystems are suppressed. A classical-quantum state is of the form
\rho_{AZ}=\sum_{z\in Z}\ket{z}\bra{z}\otimes\rho_{A}^{z}
,
where
\left\{{\ket{z}}\right\}_{z\in Z}
is an orthonormal basis for the classical register
Z
. For a quantum state
\rho_{AB}
and a POVM element
M_{B}^{z}
indexed in outcome
z\in Z
to POVM

(22)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{B\rightarrow Z}\,:\,\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})&\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{Z})\\
\rho_{B}&\rightarrow\sum_{z\in Z}\mathrm{tr}\left[{M_{B}^{z}\rho_{B}}\right]\ket{z}\bra{z}_{Z},\,\end{split}

we denote by

(23)

\displaystyle\rho_{A|z}=\frac{\mathrm{tr}_{Z}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes M_{B}^{z}}\right)\rho_{AB}}\right]}{\mathrm{tr}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes M_{B}^{z}}\right)\rho_{AB}}\right]}

the post-measurement state obtained after measuring
\rho_{AB}
and obtaining outcome
z
. When the probability distribution
p(z)
is clear from context, we adopt the shorthand notation
\mathbb{E}_{z}
to denote the expectation over
z
, i.e.
\mathbb{E}_{z}\left\{{\cdot}\right\}=\sum_{z\in Z}p(z)(\cdot)
. In particular, the marginal state on system
A
can be expressed as
\rho_{A}=\mathbb{E}_{z}\left\{{\rho_{A|z}}\right\}
. The Schatten 1-norm is denoted by
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{1}
. The symmetric group on symbols
n\in\mathbb{N}
is denoted by
S_{n}
. For notational convenience, we often do not distinguish between abstract group elements and their associated representations on vector spaces; the context will make the meaning unambiguous.
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
admits a natural (left) action of
S_{n}
given by

(24)

\displaystyle\pi\cdot\left({\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}}\right)=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}h_{\pi^{-1}(i)},\,\hskip 14.22636pth_{i}\in\mathcal{H},\forall\pi\in S_{n}.\,

We denote the unitary representation

(25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}U_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{n}}\,:\,S_{n}&\rightarrow\mathcal{B}\left({\mathcal{H}_{1}^{n}}\right)\\
\pi&\mapsto U_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{n}}\left({\pi}\right)\simeq\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\in\left[{d_{\mathcal{H}}}\right]}\ket{i_{\pi^{-1}(1)},\ldots,i_{\pi^{-1}(n)}}\bra{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}\,,\end{split}

mapping permutations to real-valued permutation matrices. We reserve this notation for permutation representations of the symmetric group. Observe that, owing to the tensor-product structure of the underlying Hilbert space and the factorized action of permutation matrices on computational basis vectors, the unitary representation decomposes as
U_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)=U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)\otimes U_{\bar{B}_{1}^{n}}(\pi)
. An operator
X_{B_{1}^{n}}\in\mathcal{B}\left({B_{1}^{n}}\right)
is symmetric, if

(26)

\displaystyle U_{B_{1}^{n}}\left({\pi}\right)X_{B_{1}^{n}}U^{T}_{B_{1}^{n}}\left({\pi}\right)=X_{B_{1}^{n}},\,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\pi\in S_{n}.\,

Furthermore,
X_{AB_{1}^{n}}\in\mathcal{B}\left({AB_{1}^{n}}\right)
is symmetric w.r.t.
A
, if

(27)

\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{B_{1}^{n}}\left({\pi}\right)}\right)X_{AB_{1}^{n}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U^{T}_{B_{1}^{n}}\left({\pi}\right)}\right)=X_{AB_{1}^{n}},\,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\pi\in S_{n}.\,

In general for a group
G
, the algebra of all
G
-equivariant maps from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathcal{H}
is

(28)

\displaystyle\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)=\left\{{T\in\text{End}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\,\mid\,T(g\cdot v)=g\cdot T(v),\,\forall g\in G,\,\forall v\in\mathcal{H}}\right\}.

We will often abbreviate a group representation
\rho:G\rightarrow\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
by
g\cdot v
for any
g\in G
and
v\in\mathcal{H}
. We drop the group algebra to the trivial group action on Hilbert space
A
and simply write
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({A\otimes B_{1}^{n}}\right)
denoting the space of endomorphisms on
A\otimes B_{1}^{n}
which are
S_{n}
-equivariant w.r.t.
A
. For an introduction to additional relevant mathematical concepts, we refer the reader to
App. F
.
Furthermore, we define the minimal distortion

(29)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(A,B)&:=\inf_{\mathcal{M}_{A},\mathcal{M}_{B}}\,\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta_{AB}\in\mathcal{B}\left({\mathcal{H}_{AB}}\right)\\
\zeta_{AB}^{\dagger}=\zeta_{AB}\\
\zeta_{A}=0\ \zeta_{B}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{\|\zeta_{AB}\|_{1}}{\|(\mathcal{M}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{B}(\zeta_{AB})\|_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{watrous2018theory}{}{}]}}}}{{\leq}}18\sqrt{d_{A}d_{B}}\end{split}

where,
\mathcal{M}_{A},\,\mathcal{M}_{B}
are appropriate informationally complete measurements and minimal distortion with side information

(30)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(B|\cdot)&:=\inf_{\mathcal{M}_{B}}\,\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta_{AB}\in\mathcal{B}\left({\mathcal{H}_{AB}}\right)\\
\zeta_{AB}^{\dagger}=\zeta_{AB}\\
\zeta_{A}=0\ \zeta_{B}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{\|\zeta_{AB}\|_{1}}{\|(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{B}(\zeta_{AB})\|_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{jee2020quasi}{}{}]}}}}{{\leq}}2d_{B}.\,\end{split}

3.2.
Approximating non-local games

Our first line of attack is an equivalent formulation of
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
in
Eq. 11
in terms of block-diagonal matrices, by introducing classical systems
A_{i},Q_{i}
equipped with orthonormal bases
\{(a_{i})_{j}\}_{j=1,\ldots\lvert A_{i}\rvert,i}
,
\{(b_{i})_{j}\}_{j=1,\ldots\lvert Q_{i}\rvert,i}
with
i=1,2
and tensoring them to the quantum systems. We choose to normalize
D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}
, but not
E_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}
, and obtain

(31)

\displaystyle\begin{split}w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)=&\lvert T\rvert\max_{(\rho,\,E,\,D)}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(V_{A_{1}A_{2}Q_{1}Q_{2}}\otimes\rho_{T\hat{T}}\right)\left(E_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)\right]\\
\text{s.t.}\hskip 42.67912pt&\rho_{T\hat{T}}\succcurlyeq 0,\,\hskip 28.45274pt\mathrm{tr}\left[\rho_{T\hat{T}}\right]=1,\,\\
&E_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}=\sum_{a_{1},q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{a_{1}q_{1}}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\otimes E_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\succcurlyeq 0,\,\\
&E_{Q_{1}T}=\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{T},\,\\
&D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}=\sum_{a_{2},q_{2}}\pi_{2}(q_{2})\ket{a_{2}q_{2}}\bra{a_{2}q_{2}}_{A_{2}Q_{2}}\otimes\frac{D_{\hat{T}}(a_{2}|q_{2})}{\lvert\hat{T}\rvert}\succcurlyeq 0,\,\\
&D_{Q_{2}\hat{T}}=\sum_{q_{2}}\pi_{2}(q_{2})\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}_{Q_{2}}\otimes\frac{\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}{\lvert\hat{T}\rvert},\,\\
\end{split}

where
V_{A_{1}A_{2}Q_{1}Q_{2}}:=\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}}\sum_{a_{1},a_{2}}V(a_{1},a_{2},q_{1},q_{2})\ket{a_{1},a_{2},q_{1},q_{2}}\bra{a_{1},a_{2},q_{1},q_{2}}_{A_{1}A_{2}Q_{1}Q_{2}}
is a diagonal matrix with entries given by the rule function. We reformulate the problem above into the following variant of a cSEP problem.

Lemma 3.1
.

The value of a two-player free non-local game with
\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert
many answers
\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert
many questions and quantum assistance of size
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert
is given by the following bipartite constrained separability problem

(32)

\displaystyle\begin{split}w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)=&\lvert T\rvert\max_{(\alpha,D)}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(V_{A_{1}A_{2}Q_{1}Q_{2}}\otimes S_{\tilde{T}\hat{T}}\right)\left(\alpha_{A_{1}Q_{1}\tilde{T}}\otimes D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)\right]\\
\\
\text{s.t. }&\,\alpha_{A_{1}Q_{1}\tilde{T}}=\sum_{a_{1},q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{a_{1}q_{1}}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\otimes\alpha_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})\succcurlyeq 0,\,\\
&\alpha_{Q_{1}\tilde{T}}=\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\alpha_{\tilde{T}},\,\hskip 28.45274pt\mathrm{tr}\left[{\alpha_{A_{1}Q_{1}\tilde{T}}}\right]=1\\
&D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}=\sum_{a_{2},q_{2}}\pi_{2}(q_{2})\ket{a_{2}q_{2}}\bra{a_{2}q_{2}}_{A_{2}Q_{2}}\otimes\frac{D_{\hat{T}}(a_{2}|q_{2})}{\lvert T\rvert}\succcurlyeq 0,\,\\
&D_{Q_{2}\hat{T}}=\sum_{q_{2}}\pi_{2}(q_{2})\ket{q_{2}}\bra{q_{2}}_{Q_{2}}\otimes\frac{\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}{\lvert T\rvert}.\,\\
\end{split}

where
S_{\tilde{T}\hat{T}}
is the swap-operator and
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{\tilde{T}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{\hat{T}}\right\rvert
.

The proof is deferred to
App. B
. We observe that the optimization over
\alpha_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}
can be equivalently reformulated as an optimization over a finite convex mixture of these terms. It is important to emphasize that this yields an equivalent optimization problem only if the constraints are enforced individually for each summand in the mixture (see
App. A
for additional details). Crucially, our reformulation as a bipartite problem circumvents several challenges inherent to the tripartite formulation analyzed in
[
Jee+21
]
. However, the constraints appearing in
Eq. 32
exhibit a more intricate structure than those considered in
[
Jee+21
]
, or indeed in any previously studied instances within the cSEP framework. To accommodate these more complex constraints, we introduce a broader class of cSEP problems and extend the methodological framework originally developed in
[
Ber+21
]
to accommodate them. A detailed discussion of this extension is provided in
App. A
.

Following
[
Ber+21
]
, we establish the connection to an SDP hierarchy of outer approximations. Finally, we conclude by proving the convergence of this hierarchy. As any separable state admits an
n
-symmetric extension for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can formulate a hierarchy of necessary conditions in terms of symmetric states. Concretely, the bipartite state
\rho_{\left({A_{1}Q_{1}T}\right)\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)}
is said to be
n
-extendable if there exists a state
\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}
, which is symmetric over
(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}
w.r.t.
(A_{1}Q_{1}T)
and that upon tracing out any
n-1
of the copy-equivalent
(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{i}
we recover
\rho_{\left({A_{1}Q_{1}T}\right)\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)}
. We can identify
(A_{1}Q_{1}T)
with Alice and
(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}
with
n
Bobs. Furthermore,
\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}
satisfies adapted constraints that encode the positivity requirement and the non-signaling condition between Alice and each of the
n
Bobs. In summary, we obtain

(33)

\displaystyle\begin{split}SDP_{n}&\left({T,V,\pi}\right)=\lvert T\rvert\cdot\max_{\rho}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(V_{A_{1}A_{2}Q_{1}Q_{2}}\otimes S_{T\hat{T}}\right)\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})}\right],\,\\
\end{split}

subject to the
n
-extendability conditions

(34)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})}&=\mathrm{tr}_{(A_{1}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{2}^{n}}\left[\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\right],\,\hskip 14.22636pt\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\succeq 0,\,\hskip 14.22636pt\mathrm{tr}\left[\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\right]=1,\,\\
\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}&=\left({\mathcal{I}_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes U_{\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\left({\pi}\right)}\right)\left(\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes U_{\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}^{T}\left({\pi}\right)}\right)\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\pi\in S_{n},\,\\
\end{split}

and additional linear constraints

(35)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{tr}_{A_{1}}\left[{\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}}\right]&=\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}\otimes\rho_{T\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}},\,\\
\mathrm{tr}_{(A_{2})_{1}}\left[\rho_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\right]&=\left(\sum_{q_{2}}\pi_{2}(q_{2})\ket{q_{2}}\bra{q_{2}}_{(Q_{2})_{1}}\otimes\frac{\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}_{1}}}{\lvert T\rvert}\right)\otimes\rho_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{2}^{n}}.\,\\
\end{split}

The rate of convergence at which
SDP_{n}(T,V,\pi)
works to approximate
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
from above follows from a de Finetti representation theorem-type argument. However, due to the new constraints, we need an extension of
[
Ber+21
, Theorem 2.3]
to accommodate our setting. See
Lem. A.5
for this new variant of a constrained de Finetti theorem.

Proof of
Lem. 2.1
.

By the constrained de Finetti representation theorem in
Lem. A.5
, there exists a state
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\,\rho^{x}_{\left({A_{1}Q_{1}T}\right)}\otimes\rho^{x}_{\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)}
feasible to
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)
in
Eq. 32
, such that

(36)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{\left({A_{1}Q_{1}T}\right)\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)}-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\rho^{x}_{\left({A_{1}Q_{1}T}\right)}\otimes\rho^{X}_{\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)}\right\rVert_{1}\leq 2\cdot\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert\sqrt{4\ln 2}\sqrt{\frac{\log\left({\left\lvert{A_{1}}\right\rvert\left\lvert{Q_{1}}\right\rvert\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right)}{n}}\,.

By Hölder’s inequality,

(37)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lvert SDP_{n}(T,V,\pi)-w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)\right\rvert&\leq\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lVert\rho_{\left({A_{1}Q_{1}T}\right)\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)}-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p_{(}x)\rho^{x}_{\left({A_{1}Q_{1}T}\right)}\otimes\rho^{x}_{\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)}\right\rVert_{1}\\
&\leq 2\cdot\lvert T\rvert^{3}\sqrt{4\ln 2}\sqrt{\frac{\log\left({\left\lvert{A_{1}}\right\rvert\left\lvert{Q_{1}}\right\rvert\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right)}{n}},\,\end{split}

and hence,
w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}SDP_{n}(T,V,\pi)
. In summary, to determine the value of a non-local free game with fixed quantum assistance up to an additive
\epsilon
-error requires

(38)

\displaystyle\begin{split}n&\geq\frac{16\ln 2\cdot\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert^{6}\log\left({\left\lvert{A_{1}}\right\rvert\left\lvert{Q_{1}}\right\rvert\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right)}{\epsilon^{2}}\end{split}

many steps in the hierarchy. For
\left\lvert{A_{1}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{A_{2}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert
and
\left\lvert{Q_{1}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{Q_{2}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert
this corresponds to solving an SDP with variables of size
\left({\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right)^{\mathcal{O}\left({\frac{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert^{6}\log\left({\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right)}{\epsilon^{2}}}\right)}
, which is

(39)

\displaystyle\exp\left[{\frac{\mathcal{O}\left({\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert^{6}\log^{2}\left\lvert{AQT}\right\rvert}\right)}{\epsilon^{2}}}\right].\,

∎

4.
Convergent sequence of inner bounds to constrained separability problems

To establish the rounding result in the context of non-local games, we first prove it within the more general cSEP framework.

Lemma 4.1
.

Consider
cSEP(G)
of the form given in
Eq. 12
. Let
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}^{*}
be optimal to
\text{SDP}_{n}(G)
approximating
cSEP(G)
from above. Then, there exists a rounding procedure mapping
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}^{*}
to
\sigma_{AB}
feasible in
cSEP(G)
, such that

(40)

\displaystyle\left\lvert{cSEP(G)-\mathrm{tr}\left[{G_{AB}\sigma_{AB}}\right]}\right\rvert\leq\min\{f(A|B),f(B|\cdot)\}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln 2\log\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert}{n}}\,.

Proof.

Let
M_{B_{1}^{m}}
be an informationally-complete measurement mapping
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}^{*}
to
\rho_{AZ_{1}^{m}B_{m+1}^{n}}
. Consider the conditional marginal states obtained after having measured with POVM element
M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}
followed by tracing out
Z_{1}^{m}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}=\frac{\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+1}^{n}}\left[\left({\mathcal{I}_{AB_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}^{*}\right]}{\mathrm{tr}\left[{\left(\mathcal{I}_{AB_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}\right)\left(\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}^{*}\right)}\right]}\,,&\rho_{B_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}=\frac{\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+2}^{n}}\left[{\left(\mathcal{I}_{B_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}\right)\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}^{*}}\right]}{\mathrm{tr}\left[{\left(\mathcal{I}_{B_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}\right)\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}^{*}}\right]}.\\
\end{array}

By the same arguments as in the proof of
Lem. A.5
, for every
z_{1}^{m}
and every
m\in\left[{n}\right]
, we have

(42)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}|z_{1}^{m}},&\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}}\right)=\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}},\\
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{m+1}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\rho_{B_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{\left({B_{R}}\right)_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}},&\Xi_{B_{m+1}\rightarrow B_{m+1}}\left({\rho_{B_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}}\right)=\rho_{B_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}.\\
\end{array}\end{split}

Then,
\sum_{z_{1}^{m}}p\left({z_{1}^{m}}\right)\,\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}\otimes\rho_{B_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}
is a feasible point to
cSEP(G)
. Starting with
m=1
and, for each
m=2,\,3,\ldots,\,n
, iteratively applying the construction that yields the states described in
Sec. 4
, the constrained quantum de Finetti theorem in
Lem. A.5
guarantees the existence of some
k\in\left[{n}\right]
, such that

\displaystyle\lVert\rho_{AB_{1}}^{*}-\sum_{z_{1}^{k}}p\left({z_{1}^{k}}\right)\,\rho_{A|z_{1}^{k}}\otimes\rho_{B_{k+1}|z_{1}^{k}}\rVert_{1}\leq\min\{f(A|B),f(B|\cdot)\}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln 2\log_{2}d_{A}}{n}}.\,

where the minimal distortions
f(A|B),f(B|\cdot)
can be bounded for informationally-complete measurements. Furthermore, since
SDP_{n}(G)-cSEP(G)\geq 0
, and by Hölder’s inequality

(43)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\lvert{cSEP(G)-\max_{z_{1}^{k}}\left\{{\mathrm{tr}\left[{G_{AB}\,\left({\rho_{A|z_{1}^{k}}\otimes\rho_{B_{k+1}|z_{1}^{k}}}\right)}\right]}\right\}_{z_{1}^{k}}}\right\rvert\\
&\leq\left\lvert{cSEP(G)-\mathrm{tr}\left[{G_{AB}\,\left({\sum_{z_{1}^{k}}p\left({z_{1}^{k}}\right)\,\rho_{A|z_{1}^{k}}\otimes\rho_{B_{k+1}|z_{1}^{k}}}\right)}\right]}\right\rvert\\
&\leq\left\lvert{SDP_{n}(G)-\mathrm{tr}\left[{G_{AB}\left({\sum_{z_{1}^{k}}p\left({z_{1}^{k}}\right)\,\rho_{A|z_{1}^{k}}\otimes\rho_{B_{k+1}|z_{1}^{k}}}\right)}\right]}\right\rvert\leq\min\{f(A|B),f(B|\cdot)\}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln 2\log_{2}d_{A}}{n}}\,.\end{split}

∎

While employing informationally complete measurements guarantees the convergence rate of the hierarchy of inner bounds, measuring the
B
-systems with alternative measurement schemes also yields feasible states for
cSEP(G)
, corresponding to lower bounds on
cSEP(G)
. Consequently, in practical applications, heuristically chosen measurement strategies with less measurement outcomes may provide tighter bounds on
cSEP(G)
at finite levels of the hierarchy or simply easier to compute rounding procedures. Note, while in
Prop. A.6
we were able to formulate a hierarchy, i.e. the feasible set to
SDP_{n+1}(G)
is a subset to the feasible set to
SDP_{n}(G)
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and the feasible set to
cSEP(G)
lies densely in the limit set to
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}SDP_{n}(G)
, an according set structure cannot be given in the case of the inner sequence. Concretely, the optimal points
\rho_{AB}^{*}
to
SDP_{n+1}(G)
and
\sigma_{AB_{1}}^{*}
to
SDP_{n}(G)
are not in general in a partial trace relation.

The proof of
Thm. 2.4
follows directly from
Lem. 4.1
. Note, however, that due to the steering procedure in
Sec. 2.1
, opposed to
[
BH17
,
Kem+11
]
, the rounded strategy is not classical but quantum.

5.
Bose-symmetric hierarchy for constrained separability problems

5.1.
Introduction

The Bose-symmetric operators rely heavily on the well-known notion of the symmetric subspace described in detail in, e.g.,
[
Har13
]
. In the following, we first introduce in
Sec. 5.2
the minimal tools needed for our constrained Bose-symmetric de Finetti theorem (
Sec. 5.3
), the corresponding SDP hierarchy (
Sec. 5.4
), its computational complexity (
Sec. 5.5
), and application to non-local games (
Sec. 5.6
). We present in more mathematical detail concepts related to the symmetric subspace in
App. C
and provide the reader with general information about the relevant representation theory in
App. F
. This is meant as a thorough discussion related to the literature
[
Har13
,
Chr+07
,
FH04
,
Eti+11
,
Ste16
]
, which will in particular become crucial for
Sec. 6
.

5.2.
Bose-symmetry

Although Bose-symmetry applies to a broader class of self-adjoint operators, we restrict our attention here to quantum states. We now collect several basic facts about Bose-symmetric states that will be used repeatedly in what follows.

Definition 5.1

(Symmetric subspace)
.

The symmetric subspace is defined as

(44)

\displaystyle\lor^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right):=\left\{{\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\,:\,U_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{n}}(\pi)\ket{\psi}=\ket{\psi},\,\forall\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\right\}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\left\{{\ket{\psi}^{\otimes n}\,:\,\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}}\right\}

and the orthogonal projector onto
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
is

(45)

\displaystyle P_{\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}:=\frac{1}{\left\lvert{S_{n}}\right\rvert}\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}U_{\mathcal{H}_{1}^{n}}(\pi).\,

Definition 5.2

(Bose-symmetry)
.

A state
\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}
is Bose-symmetric, if

(46)

\displaystyle U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}=\rho_{B_{1}^{n}},\,\forall\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{n}.\,

Moreover, the state
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
is Bose-symmetric w.r.t.
A
, if

(47)

\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}=\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}},\,\forall\pi\in\mathcal{S}_{n}.\,

We state and prove the subsequent statements in terms of the stronger notion of (Bose)-symmetry w.r.t. to side system
A
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
be a state Bose-symmetric w.r.t.
A
. Then,
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
is symmetric w.r.t.
A
.

Proof.

With
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}=\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}^{*}
and unitary
U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)
for every
\pi\in S_{n}
,
\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}=\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
implies
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U^{T}_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)=\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
. Thus,

(48)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}=\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\\
&\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U^{T}_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)=\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U^{T}_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)=\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}.\,\end{split}

∎

Remark 5.4
.

The identity operator serves as an example of a symmetric operator that is not Bose-symmetric.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\in\mathcal{S}\left({AB_{1}^{n}}\right)
. Then,
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({A\otimes\vee^{n}\left({B}\right)}\right)
, if and only if
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
is Bose-symmetric w.r.t.
A
.

Proof.

By definition, any
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({A\otimes\vee^{n}\left({B}\right)}\right)
is invariant under the
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-action and, consequently, symmetric w.r.t.
A
. Moreover, for any
\psi\in A\otimes\vee^{n}\left({B}\right)
, we have

(49)

\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\psi=\psi,\,\hskip 28.45274pt\forall\pi\in S_{n}.\,

Since
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
has support and range on
A\otimes\vee^{n}\left({B}\right)
, we have
\forall\pi\in S_{n}
:

(50)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}(\psi)&=\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U^{T}_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\psi\\
&=\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U^{T}_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\psi\\
&=\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\psi=\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}(\psi).\,\end{split}

Conversely, ignoring
A
for now, let
\psi\in B_{1}^{n}
, then

(51)

\displaystyle\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}(\psi)=P_{\vee^{n}(B)}\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}(\psi)=\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}P_{\vee^{n}(B)}(\psi)

and since
P_{\vee^{n}(B)}(\psi)\in\vee^{n}\left({B}\right)
,
\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}
has support on
\vee^{n}\left({B}\right)
. Thus, with
\phi\in\vee^{n}\left({B}\right)
, we have

(52)

\displaystyle\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}(\phi)=P_{\vee^{n}(B)}\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}(\phi)

and
\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}
has range on
\vee^{n}(B)
. The implication
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({A\otimes\vee^{n}(B)}\right)
follows trivially.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

Note that
\text{End}\left({\vee^{n}(B)}\right)=\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}(B)}\right)
. See
Prop. C.10
for further details.

5.3.
A de Finetti representation theorem for constrained Bose-symmetric states

We now prove the following lemma approximating the set of constrained separable states via constrained Bose-symmetric states.

Lemma 5.7

(Constrained De Finetti representation theorem for Bose-symmetric states (restated))
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{A}=\mathcal{H}_{A_{L}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{R}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}=\mathcal{H}_{B_{L}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B_{R}}
be Hilbert spaces and
\left\lvert{B}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{\bar{B}}\right\rvert
. Moreover, let
\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}
be Bose-symmetric with respect to
A
. Furthermore, consider linear mappings
\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}},\,\Omega_{A\rightarrow A},\,\Upsilon_{B_{L}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}},\,\Xi_{B\rightarrow B}
and operators
W_{C_{A_{L}}},\,K_{C_{B_{L}}}
. Assuming

(53)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}},&\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}},\\
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{n}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\circ\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{B}_{n}}\left({\rho_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n-1}\left({B_{R}}\right)_{n}},&\Xi_{B_{n}\rightarrow B_{n}}\circ\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{B}_{n}}\left({\rho_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\rho_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n-1}B_{n}},\\
\end{array}\end{split}

then there exists a probability distribution
\left\{{p(x)}\right\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
and states
\left\{{\sigma^{x}_{A}}\right\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}},\,\left\{{\sigma^{x}_{B\bar{B}}}\right\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
such that

(54)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)}-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\sigma_{A}^{x}\otimes\sigma^{x}_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\min\left\{{f(A|B\bar{B}),f(B\bar{B}|\cdot)}\right\}\sqrt{4\ln(2)}\sqrt{\frac{\ln(\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert)}{n}}

where
f(A|B\bar{B}),f(B\bar{B}|\cdot)
are minimal measurement distortions and for each
x\in\mathcal{X}

(55)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\sigma^{x}_{A}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\sigma^{x}_{A_{R}},&\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\sigma^{x}_{A}}\right)=\sigma^{x}_{A},\\
\Upsilon_{B_{L}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\circ\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{B}}\left({\sigma^{x}_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\sigma^{x}_{B_{R}},&\Xi_{B\rightarrow B_{n}}\circ\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{B}}\left({\sigma^{x}_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)}}\right)=\sigma^{x}_{B}.\\
\end{array}\end{split}

Proof.

With additional arguments from
Sec. 5.2
, the proof technique from
[
Ber+21
, Theorem 2.3]
extends to the constrained Bose-symmetric case. Consider a measurement
\mathcal{M}_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{m}}
with POVM elements indexed in outcomes
z_{1}^{m}
mapping
\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}
to
\rho_{AZ_{1}^{n}}
. Since
\rho_{AZ_{1}^{n}}
is symmetric w.r.t.
A
, the self-decoupling lemma
[
BH16
, Lemma 2.1]
certifies the existence of an
m\in\left[{n}\right]
, such that

(56)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{z_{1}^{m}}\left\lVert\rho_{AZ_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}-\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}\otimes\rho_{Z_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\frac{2\ln 2\log\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert}{n}.\,

By convexity,

(57)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert\rho_{A(B\bar{B})_{m+1}}-\sum_{z_{1}^{m}}p(z_{1}^{m})\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}\otimes\rho_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\min\left\{{f(A|B\bar{B}),f(B\bar{B}|\cdot)}\right\}\sqrt{4\ln(2)}\sqrt{\frac{\ln(\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert)}{n}}.\,\\
\end{split}

where the measurement distortions can be bounded by
[
Wat18
, Theorem 3.39]
or
[
Jee+21
, Lemma 8]
for informationally-complete measurement mapping
\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{m+1}}
to
\rho_{AZ_{m+1}}
and
\rho_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{m+1}}
to
\rho_{Z_{m+1}}
. Up to this point, the bound on the
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{1}
-norm follows verbatim from the proof of
Lem. A.5
and remains independent of the specific linear constraints. We now show that, for each
z_{1}^{m}
, the candidate product states satisfy the linear constraints. Subsequently, let
\frac{1}{N}
denote the corresponding normalization factor of the post measurement state. Thus, due to the commutativity of Alice’s maps with
\mathcal{M}_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{m}}
and the assumptions of the theorem, we obtain

(58)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}}\right)&=\frac{1}{N}\,\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}\left({B\bar{B}_{m+1}^{n}}\right)}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes M_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{m+1}^{n}}}\right)\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right]}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\,\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}\left({B\bar{B}_{m+1}^{n}}\right)}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{C_{A_{L}}A_{R}}\otimes M_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{m+1}^{n}}}\right)\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{eqn:SDP_constraints_appendix}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{N}\,\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}\left({B\bar{B}_{m+1}^{n}}\right)}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{C_{A}A_{R}}\otimes M_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{m+1}^{n}}}\right)W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right]\\
&=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}|z_{1}^{m}}\end{split}

and

(59)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}}\right)&=\frac{1}{N}\,\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}\left({B\bar{B}_{m+1}^{n}}\right)}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes M_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{m+1}^{n}}}\right)\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right]}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\,\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}\left({B\bar{B}_{m+1}^{n}}\right)}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes M_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{m+1}^{n}}}\right)\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{eqn:SDP_constraints_appendix}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{N}\,\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}\left({B\bar{B}_{m+1}^{n}}\right)}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes M_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{m+1}^{n}}}\right)\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right]=\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}\end{split}

Owing to the presence of a copy system on Bob’s side, establishing this implication for his constraints requires additional arguments beyond those used in
Lem. A.5
. Concretely, due to the symmetry of
\rho_{A\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{n}}
,
Prop. A.4
certifies the symmetry of the marginals
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
and
\rho_{A\bar{B}_{1}^{n}}
. Thus,
\mathrm{tr}_{\left({\bar{B}}\right)_{m+1}}\left[{\cdot}\right]
acts as
\mathrm{tr}_{\left({\bar{B}}\right)_{n}}\left[{\cdot}\right]
for any
m\in\left[{n}\right]
. Consider,
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{m+1}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}(\cdot)
. Again, because
\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}
is symmetric,
Prop. A.4
implies that the reduced state
\rho_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{1}^{n}}
is symmetric. We conclude that
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{m+1}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}
acts in the same way as
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{n}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}
. Thus, the commutativity of this map with
\mathcal{M}_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{1}^{m}}
yields

(60)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{m+1}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}&\circ\mathrm{tr}_{\left({\bar{B}}\right)_{m+1}}\left[{\rho_{\left({B\bar{B}}\right)_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}}\right]\\
