Appendix A
Constrained separability problems

Constrained separability problems are pervasive in quantum information theory and constitute a subclass of the constrained bilinear optimization problems. For instance, they arise in the context of jointly constrained semidefinite bilinear programming, as developed by
[
HKT20
]
, which utilizes non-commutative extensions of the classical branch-and-bound algorithm, and in the unbounded variants described in
[
BFS16
]
. In this work, we adopt and adapt the formulation presented in
[
Ber+21
,
Ohs+24
]
.

Definition A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{A}=\mathcal{H}_{A_{L}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{R}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}=\mathcal{H}_{B_{L}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B_{R}}
be Hilbert spaces. The composite system is described by
\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
. For a cost Hamiltonian
G\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})
, consider the constrained optimization over product states

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllll}&&&\displaystyle Q^{*}(G):=\max_{\rho_{AB}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})}\mathrm{tr}[G_{AB}(\rho_{AB})]\\
&&&\\
\mathbf{\tilde{Q}(G):}&\text{s.t.}&&\text{Product states:}&\\
&&&\\
&&&\rho_{AB}=\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B},&\\
&&&\rho_{A}\succeq 0,&\rho_{B}\succeq 0,\\
&&&\mathrm{tr}\left[\rho_{A}\right]=1,&\mathrm{tr}\left[\rho_{B}\right]=1,\\
&&&&\\
&&&\text{Alice's linear constraints:}&\\
&&&&\\
&&&\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{A}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}},&\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{A}}\right)=\rho_{A},\\
\\
&&&\text{Bob's linear constraints:}&\\
&&&&\\
&&&\Upsilon_{B_{L}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\rho_{A}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{B_{R}},&\Xi_{B\rightarrow B}\left({\rho_{B}}\right)=\rho_{B},\\
\end{array}

where the dependence on
G
symbolizes the degrees of freedom in specifying the cost Hamiltonian
G_{AB}
, the linear maps, the fixed operators
X_{C_{A}},W_{C_{A_{L}}},Y_{C_{B}},K_{C_{B_{L}}}
and the dimensions involved to a specific problem under consideration.

Due to the algebraic structure of
\mathbf{\tilde{Q}(G)}
, we can equivalently optimize over the convex hull of product states along the
A:B
-partitioning, i.e. the set of separable states.

Proposition A.2

(Constrained Separability problem)
.

Consider the bilinear optimisation problem

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllll}&&&\displaystyle cSEP(G):=\max_{\rho_{AB}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})}\mathrm{tr}[G_{AB}(\rho_{AB})]\\
&&&&\\
\mathbf{cSEP(G):}&\text{s.t.}&&\text{Separable states:}&\\
&&&\\
&&&\rho_{AB}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\rho^{x}_{A}\otimes\rho^{x}_{B},&\text{s.th. }\forall x\in\mathcal{X}:\\
&&&\rho^{x}_{A}\succeq 0,\,\rho^{x}_{B}\succeq 0,&\mathrm{tr}\left[\rho^{x}_{A}\right]=1,\,\mathrm{tr}\left[\rho^{x}_{B}\right]=1\\
&&&&\\
&&&\text{Alice's linear constraints $\forall x\in\mathcal{X}$:}&\\
&&&&\\
&&&\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho^{x}_{A}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho^{x}_{A_{R}}&\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho^{x}_{A}}\right)=\rho^{x}_{A},\\
\\
&&&\text{Bob's linear constraints $\forall x\in\mathcal{X}$:}&\\
&&&&\\
&&&\Upsilon_{B_{L}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\rho^{x}_{B}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho^{x}_{B_{R}},&\Xi_{B\rightarrow B}\left({\rho^{x}_{B}}\right)=\rho^{x}_{B}.\\
\end{array}

Then,
\mathbf{cSEP(G)}
and
\mathbf{\tilde{Q}(G)}
are equivalent.

Proof.

We omit a complete proof, as the reasoning mirrors that of
[
Ber+21
,
Bor22
]
; the additional constraint does not interfere with the structure or validity of the original argument.
∎

While
\mathbf{cSEP(G)}
, as opposed to
\mathbf{\tilde{Q}(G)}
, constitutes an optimisation over a convex set, characterizing this set efficiently and thus solving
\mathbf{cSEP(G)}
is NP-hard. Hence, in
[
BFS16
,
Ber+19
,
Ber+21
]
the authors provide a hierarchy of outer approximation to
\mathbf{cSEP(G)}
yielding upper bounds on
cSEP(G)
. Concretely, as in the DPS hierarchy, the set of separable states can be approximated by its superset of
n-
extendable states w.r.t. system
A
, denoted
n-
Ext
(A:B)
. While quantum generalizations of classical de Finetti representation theorems such as
[
Chr+07
,
CFS02
]
proved that this approximation is tight for
n\rightarrow\infty
, as well as quantifying the corresponding rate of convergence, they do not show that the resulting states satisfy the linear constraints in
\mathbf{cSEP(G)}
. More precisely, the constructed measure in the de Finetti theorem defined on state space need not vanish (approximately zero) on the subset of states not fulfilling these linear constraints. Hence, in order to prove convergence via a quantum de Finetti representation theorem
[
Ber+19
,
Ber+21
]
provided an adapted finite de Finetti representation theorem, which ensures this condition via information-theoretic arguments. We extend their result to accommodate additional constraints.

Remark A.3
.

By choosing
A_{R}
and
B_{R}
to be one-dimensional, we recover the special case considered in
[
Ber+21
,
Jee+21
,
Ohs+24
]
.

Proposition A.4
.

Let
\rho_{A\left({B_{L}B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}
by symmetric w.r.t.
A
. Then, the marginal
\rho_{A\left({B_{L}B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}
is also symmetric w.r.t.
A
.

Proof.

This follows from a straightforward computation:

(195)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\rho_{A\left({B_{L}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U^{T}_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\\
\hskip 28.45274pt&=\mathrm{tr}_{\left({B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\left[{\left({U_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)\otimes\mathcal{I}_{A\left({B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)\rho_{A\left({B_{L}B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\left({U^{T}_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)\otimes\mathcal{I}_{A\left({B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)}\right]\\
&=\mathrm{tr}_{\left({B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)\otimes U^{T}_{\left({B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)U_{\left({B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\rho_{A\left({B_{L}B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U^{T}_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)\otimes\mathcal{I}_{A\left({B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right)}\right]\\
&=\mathrm{tr}_{\left({B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)\otimes U_{\left({B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\rho_{A\left({B_{L}B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U^{T}_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)\otimes U^{T}_{\left({B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)}\right]\\
&=\mathrm{tr}_{\left({B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U_{\left({B_{L}B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\rho_{A\left({B_{L}B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes U^{T}_{\left({B_{L}B_{R}}\right)_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)}\right]=\rho_{A\left({B_{L}}\right)_{1}^{n}}.\,\end{split}

∎

Lemma A.5

(De Finetti representation theorem with linear constraints (restated))
.

Let
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}^{\otimes n})
with
\mathcal{H}_{A}=\mathcal{H}_{A_{L}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{R}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}=\mathcal{H}_{B_{L}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B_{R}}
be a quantum state symmetric on
B_{1}^{n}
w.r.t.
A
. Furthermore, consider linear mappings
\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}},\,\Omega_{A\rightarrow A},\,\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{n}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}},\,\Xi_{B_{n}\rightarrow B_{n}}
and operators
W_{C_{A_{L}}},\,K_{C_{B_{L}}}
. Assuming

(196)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}B_{1}^{n}},&\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\\
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{n}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{B_{1}^{n-1}\left({B_{R}}\right)_{n}},&\Xi_{B_{n}\rightarrow B_{n}}\left({\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\rho_{B_{1}^{n}},\\
\end{array}\end{split}

then there exists a probability distribution
\left\{{p(x)}\right\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
and states
\left\{{\sigma_{A}^{x}}\right\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}},\,\left\{{\sigma_{B}^{x}}\right\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
such that the following is true

(1)

for
\rho_{AB}:=\text{tr}_{B_{2}^{n}}[\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}]
we have

\displaystyle\|\rho_{AB}-\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\sigma_{A}^{x}\otimes\omega_{B}^{x}\|_{1}\leq\min\{f(A|B),f(B|\cdot)\}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln 2\log_{2}d_{A}}{n}}

(2)

for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
we have

(197)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{array}[]{cc}\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\sigma^{x}_{A}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\sigma^{x}_{A_{R}},&\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\sigma^{x}_{A}}\right)=\sigma^{x}_{A},\\
\Upsilon_{B_{L}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\sigma^{x}_{B}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\sigma^{x}_{B_{R}},&\Xi_{B\rightarrow B}\left({\sigma^{x}_{B}}\right)=\sigma^{x}_{B}.\\
\end{array}\end{split}

Proof.

The proof technique from
[
Ber+21
, Theorem 2.3]
extends to the additional constraints. Consider a measurement
\mathcal{M}_{B_{1}^{m}}
with POVM elements indexed in outcomes
z_{1}^{m}
mapping
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
to
\rho_{AZ_{1}^{n}}
. Since
\rho_{AZ_{1}^{n}}
is symmetric w.r.t.
A
, the self-decoupling lemma
[
BH16
, Lemma 2.1]
certifies the existence of an
m\in\left[{n}\right]
, such that

(198)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{z_{1}^{m}}\left\lVert\rho_{AZ_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}-\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}\otimes\rho_{Z_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\frac{2\ln 2\log\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert}{n}.\,

By convexity, we have

(199)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{AB_{m+1}}-\sum_{z_{1}^{m}}p(z_{1}^{m})\,\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}\otimes\rho_{B_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\min\left({f(A|B),\,f(B|)\cdot}\right)\sqrt{\frac{2\ln 2\log\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert}{n}}.\,

where the measurement distortions can be bounded by
Eq. 29
or
Eq. 30
for informationally-complete measurement mapping
\rho_{AB_{m+1}}
to
\rho_{AZ_{m+1}}
. Next, we show, that the candidate state
\sum_{z_{1}^{m}}p(z_{1}^{m})\,\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}\otimes\rho_{B_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}
satisfies the new constraints. In short, all maps defined in
Eq. 196
commute with the measurement map on
B_{1}^{m}
, and Bob’s operations are symmetric w.r.t. the subsystems on which they act. Subsequently, let
\frac{1}{N}
denote the corresponding normalization factor of the post measurement state. Thus,

(200)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{A|{z_{1}^{m}}}}\right)&=\frac{1}{N}\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+1}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{AB_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right]}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+1}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{C_{A_{L}}A_{R}B_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right)}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{eqn:deFinetti_constraints_appendix}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{N}\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+1}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{C_{A_{L}}A_{R}B_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}B_{1}^{n}}}\right]=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}|z_{1}^{m}}\end{split}

and

(201)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}}\right)&=\frac{1}{N}\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+1}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{AB_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right]}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+1}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{AB_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right)}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{eqn:deFinetti_constraints_appendix}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{N}\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+1}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{AB_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right]=\rho_{A|z_{1}^{m}}\,.\end{split}

Again, let
\frac{1}{R}
denote the normalization factor in the subsequent arguments. Moreover, by exploiting the symmetry of
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
over the subsystems
B_{1}^{n}
relative to
A
, we observe that any map defined on
B_{m+1}
acts identically on
B_{n}
for any
m\in\left[{n}\right]
. Furthermore, with
Prop. A.4
, which allows us to conclude that
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{m+1}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}
acts in the same way as
\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{n}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}
, and thus,

(202)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{m+1}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\rho_{B_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}}\right)&=\frac{1}{R}\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{m+1}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+2}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{B_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right]}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{R}\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+2}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{C_{B_{L}}\left({B_{R}}\right)_{m+1}B_{m+2}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{m+1}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right)}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{eqn:deFinetti_constraints_appendix}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{R}\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+2}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{C_{B_{L}}\left({B_{R}}\right)_{m+1}B_{m+2}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)\rho_{B_{1}^{m}}\otimes K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{\left({B_{R}}\right)_{m+1}B_{m+2}^{n}}}\right]\\
&=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{\left({B_{R}}\right)_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}\end{split}

and

(203)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Xi_{B_{m+1}\rightarrow B_{m+1}}\left({\rho_{B_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}}\right)&=\frac{1}{R}\Xi_{B_{m+1}\rightarrow B_{m+1}}\left({\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+2}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{B_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right]}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{R}\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+2}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{B_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)\Xi_{B_{m+1}\rightarrow B_{m+1}}\left({\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right)}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{eqn:deFinetti_constraints_appendix}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{R}\mathrm{tr}_{Z_{1}^{m}B_{m+2}^{n}}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{B_{m+1}^{n}}\otimes M_{B_{1}^{m}}^{z_{1}^{m}}}\right)\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right]=\rho_{B_{m+1}|z_{1}^{m}}\,.\\
\end{split}

Due to symmetry, the arguments hold for any
m\in\left[{n}\right]
.
∎

Roughly speaking,
Lem. A.5
states that even under the additional assumption of linear constraints a hierarchy of outer approximations is working to approximate (
A.1
). Even more so, it provides an upper bound on the distance of the
n-
extendable state to a separable state. Importantly, each state in the separable state decomposition also adheres to the linear constraints. Using this de Finetti theorem, the constrained bilinear optimization problem (
A.1
) can be relaxed to an SDP corresponding to an optimization over an outer approximation of the set of separable states yielding an upper bound on (
A.1
). Concretely, one obtains a hierarchy of SDP relaxations indexed in the (symmetric) extension
n
of
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
with respect to
A
(i.e. approximating
\rho_{AB}\in
Sep
(A:B)
with
n
-extendable states
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
). The following theorem specifies this hierarchy and quantifies its rate of convergence.

Proposition A.6

(Hierarchy of SDP relaxations)
.

Identify
B_{1}:=B
, i.e.
\rho_{AB}=\rho_{AB_{1}}=\mathrm{tr}_{B_{2}^{n}}\left(\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\right)
.
The level
n
SDP relaxation of
\mathbf{cSEP(G)}
is given by

\displaystyle\text{SDP}_{n}^{*}(G):=\max_{\rho_{AB_{1}}}\text{tr}\left[G_{AB}\rho_{AB_{1}}\right]

subject to

(1)

\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\succeq 0
and
\mathrm{tr}[\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}]=1
,

(2)

\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}=U_{B_{1}^{n}}^{T}(\sigma)\left(\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\right)U_{B_{1}^{n}}(\sigma)\quad\forall\sigma\in S_{n}
,

(3)

\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right)=W_{C_{A_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}B_{1}^{n}}
,

(4)

\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
,

(5)

\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{n}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{B_{1}^{n-1}\left({B_{R}}\right)_{n}}
,

(6)

\Xi_{B_{n}\rightarrow B_{n}}\left({\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}
,

yielding a sequence of upper bounds on
cSEP(G)
converging from above with
n\rightarrow\infty
, i.e.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccc}\displaystyle 0\leq\text{SDP}^{*}_{n}(G)-Q^{*}(G)\leq\min\{f(A|B),f(B|\cdot)\}\sqrt{\frac{2\ln 2\log_{2}\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert}{n}}&\displaystyle.\end{array}

Proof.

Let
\rho_{AB}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\,\rho_{A}^{x}\otimes\rho_{B}^{x}
be feasible to
\mathbf{cSEP(G)}
. A separable state is
n
-extendable for any
n
. Define

(205)

\displaystyle\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}:=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\,\rho_{A}^{x}\otimes\left({\rho_{B}^{x}}\right)^{\otimes n}\,.

Then,

(206)

\displaystyle\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\,\Theta_{A_{L}\rightarrow C_{A_{L}}}\left({\rho_{A}^{x}}\right)\otimes\left({\rho_{B}^{x}}\right)^{\otimes n}=W_{A_{C_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}}^{x}\otimes\left({\rho_{B}^{x}}\right)^{\otimes n}=W_{A_{C_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{A_{R}B_{1}^{n}}

and

(207)

\displaystyle\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\,\Omega_{A\rightarrow A}\left({\rho_{A}^{x}}\right)\otimes\left({\rho_{B}^{x}}\right)^{\otimes n}=\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}\,.

Due to the exchange symmetry of
\rho_{AB_{1}^{n}}
,

(208)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)_{n}\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\,\left({\rho_{B}^{x}}\right)^{\otimes n-1}\otimes\Upsilon_{\left({B_{L}}\right)\rightarrow C_{B_{L}}}\left({\rho_{B}^{x}}\right)=K_{C_{B_{L}}}\otimes\rho_{B_{1}^{n-1}\left({B_{R}}\right)_{n}}

and

(209)

\displaystyle\Xi_{B_{n}\rightarrow B_{n}}\left({\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}}\right)=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\,\left({\rho_{B}^{x}}\right)^{\otimes n-1}\otimes\Xi_{B\rightarrow B}\left({\rho_{B}^{x}}\right)=\rho_{B_{1}^{n}}.

Conversely, the constrained de Finetti theorem in
Lem. A.5
certifies the convergence rate of
SDP_{n}(G)
to
cSEP(G)
.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of
Lem. 3.1

For completeness, we recall two relevant results from the quantum steering literature, which will be used in the subsequent proof. For an introduction to the concepts underlying quantum steering, we refer the reader to
[
NC12
]
.

Proposition B.1

(Transpose trick)
.

Let
\rho_{AB}=\sum_{i}\ket{i}_{A}\ket{i}_{B}
be the maximally entangled state on
\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
with orthonormal basis
\{\ket{i}_{A}\otimes\ket{i}_{B}\}_{i,j}
and
\text{dim}(\mathcal{H}_{A})=\text{dim}(\mathcal{H}_{B})
. Additionally, let
M\in\mathcal{B}\left(\mathcal{H}_{A}\right)
be an arbitrary operator and
M^{T}
its transposed with respect to said orthonormal basis. Then,

(210)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(M_{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{B}\right)\rho_{AB}=\left(\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes M^{T}_{B}\right)\rho_{AB}.\,\end{split}

Proposition B.2

(Swap-Trick)
.

Consider any
N,M\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
and let
S_{A\hat{A}}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\hat{A}})
be the (non-normalised) swap operator defined as
S_{A\hat{A}}:=\sum_{i,j}\ket{i}\bra{j}_{A}\otimes\ket{j}\bra{i}_{\hat{A}}
. Then,
\mathrm{tr}\left[MN\right]=\mathrm{tr}\left[S_{A\hat{A}}\left(M\otimes N\right)\right]
.

To prove
Lem. 3.1
, we first prove the following intermediate result.

Proposition B.3
.

The value of a two-player free non-local game with
\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert
many answers
\left\lvert{Q}\right\rvert
many questions and quantum assistance of size
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert
is given by the following bipartite constrained separability problem

(211)

\displaystyle\begin{split}w_{Q(T)}(V,\pi)=&\lvert T\rvert\max_{(\alpha,D)}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(V_{A_{1}A_{2}Q_{1}Q_{2}}\otimes S_{\hat{T}\tilde{T}}\right)\left(\alpha_{A_{1}Q_{1}\tilde{T}}\otimes D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)\right]\\
\\
\text{s.t. }&\alpha_{A_{1}Q_{1}\tilde{T}}=\sum_{a_{1},q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{a_{1}q_{1}}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\otimes\alpha_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})\succcurlyeq 0,\,\\
&\alpha_{Q_{1}\tilde{T}}=\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\sum_{a_{1}}\alpha_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1}=1),\,\hskip 14.22636pt\mathrm{tr}\left[{\alpha_{A_{1}Q_{1}\tilde{T}}}\right]=1,\,\\
&D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}=\sum_{a_{2},q_{2}}\pi_{2}(q_{2})\ket{a_{2}q_{2}}\bra{a_{2}q_{2}}_{A_{2}Q_{2}}\otimes\frac{D_{\hat{T}}(a_{2}|q_{2})}{\lvert T\rvert}\succcurlyeq 0,\,\\
&D_{Q_{2}\hat{T}}=\sum_{q_{2}}\pi_{2}(q_{2})\ket{q_{2}}\bra{q_{2}}_{Q_{2}}\otimes\frac{\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}{\lvert T\rvert}.\,\\
\end{split}

where
S_{\hat{T}\tilde{T}}
is the swap-operator and
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{\tilde{T}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{\hat{T}}\right\rvert
.

Proof.

Let
Eq. 31
be denoted by
\left({\mathrm{I}}\right)
and
Eq. 211
by
\left({\mathrm{II}}\right)
. Clearly, any
D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}
belonging to the feasible set of
\left({\mathrm{I}}\right)
is also a feasible point to problem
\left({\mathrm{II}}\right)
and vice versa.

Let
E_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}
and
\rho_{T\hat{T}}
be feasible points to
\left({\mathrm{I}}\right)
. Define for each
a_{1}\in A_{1}
,
q_{1}\in Q_{1}

(212)

\displaystyle\beta_{\hat{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1}):=\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({E_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}\right)\rho_{T\hat{T}}}\right]

and

(213)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{A_{1}Q_{1}\hat{T}}&:=\sum_{a_{1},q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{a_{1}q_{1}}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}}_{A_{1},Q_{1}}\otimes\beta_{\hat{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})=\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({E_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\otimes\rho_{T\hat{T}}}\right)}\right].\,\end{split}

Since
\rho_{T\hat{T}},\,E_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\succcurlyeq 0
and with the cyclicity of
\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\cdot}\right]
we have

(214)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{A_{1}Q_{1}\hat{T}}&=\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({\sum_{a_{1},q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{a_{1}q_{1}}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\otimes E_{T}\left({a_{1}|q_{1}}\right)\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\otimes\rho_{T\hat{T}}}\right)}\right]\\
&=\sum_{a_{1},q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{a_{1}q_{1}}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({E_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}\right)\rho_{T\hat{T}}}\right]\\
&=\sum_{a_{1},q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{a_{1}q_{1}}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({E_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho_{T\hat{T}}\left({E_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right]\succcurlyeq 0.\,\\
\end{split}

Furthermore, using the condition
\sum_{a_{1}}E_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})=\mathcal{I}_{T}
for all
q_{1}\in Q_{1}
, we obtain

(215)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{tr}_{A_{1}}\left[{\beta_{A_{1}Q_{1}\hat{T}}}\right]&=\mathrm{tr}_{A_{1}}\left[{\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({E_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\otimes\rho_{T\hat{T}}}\right)}\right]}\right]=\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({E_{Q_{1}T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\rho_{T\hat{T}}}\right)}\right]\\
&=\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{T\hat{T}}}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\rho_{T\hat{T}}}\right)}\right]=\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\rho_{\hat{T}}\\
&=\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\sum_{a_{1}}\beta_{\hat{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})\end{split}

and hence
\mathrm{tr}\left[{\rho_{\hat{T}}}\right]=1
implies
\mathrm{tr}\left[{\beta_{A_{1}Q_{1}\hat{T}}}\right]=1
. Since
\left\lvert{\hat{T}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{\tilde{T}}\right\rvert
, and thus,
\mathcal{H}_{\hat{T}}\simeq\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{T}}
,
\beta_{A_{1}Q_{1}\tilde{T}}:=\beta_{A_{1}Q_{1}\hat{T}}
is a feasible point to
\left({\mathrm{II}}\right)
. Since

(216)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(V_{A_{1}A_{2}Q_{1}Q_{2}}\otimes\rho_{T\hat{T}}\right)\left(E_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)\right]&=\mathrm{tr}\left[{\left({V_{A_{1}A_{2}Q_{1}Q_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}\right)\beta_{A_{1}Q_{1}\hat{T}}D_{\hat{T}}(a_{2}|q_{2})}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{prop:swap_trick_renner}}}{{=}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(V_{A_{1}A_{2}Q_{1}Q_{2}}\otimes S_{\hat{T}\tilde{T}}\right)\left(\beta_{A_{1}Q_{1}\tilde{T}}\otimes D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)\right]\end{split}

the construction in
Eq. 212
converts feasible points from
\left({\mathrm{I}}\right)
to feasible points of
\left({\mathrm{II}}\right)
with the same objective value. Hence, optimality is preserved.

Conversely, given a feasible
\alpha_{A_{1}Q_{1}\tilde{T}}
for
\left({\mathrm{II}}\right)
, we define, for each
q_{1}\in Q_{1}
,
\sigma_{\tilde{T}}^{q_{1}}:=\sum_{a_{1}}\alpha_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})
. The constraint on
\alpha_{Q_{1}\tilde{T}}
implies

\sum_{a_{1}}\alpha_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})=\sum_{a_{1}}\alpha_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q^{\prime}_{1})
for any
q^{\prime}_{1}\in Q_{1}
. Thus, we have
\sigma_{\tilde{T}}^{q_{1}}=\sigma_{\tilde{T}}^{q^{\prime}_{1}}=:\sigma_{\tilde{T}}
. Furthermore,
\alpha_{A_{1}Q_{1}\tilde{T}}\succcurlyeq 0
implies
\alpha_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})\succcurlyeq 0
for all
a_{1}\in A_{1}
and
q_{1}\in Q_{1}
. This in turn implies
\sigma_{\tilde{T}}\succcurlyeq 0
. Moreover,
\mathrm{tr}\left[{\sum_{a_{1}}\alpha_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})}\right]=1
implies
\mathrm{tr}\left[{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}\right]=1
. Let

(217)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\tilde{T}}=\sum_{k=1}^{\text{rank}\left({\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}\right)}\lambda_{k}\ket{e_{k}}\bra{e_{k}}_{\tilde{T}}

be its eigenbasis decomposition and

(218)

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi\left({\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}\right)}_{T\tilde{T}}:=\sum_{k=1}^{\text{rank}\left({\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}\right)}\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}\ket{e_{k}}_{T}\otimes\ket{e_{k}}_{\tilde{T}}

the corresponding Schmidt decomposition.
By construction,
\rho_{T\tilde{T}}^{\sigma}:=\ket{\Psi\left({\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}\right)}\bra{\Psi\left({\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}\right)}_{T\tilde{T}}
is a normalized quantum state and thus feasible in
\left({\mathrm{I}}\right)
. To determine Alice’s POVMs feasible in
\left({\mathrm{I}}\right)
we ask, which operators yield
\alpha_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})
as Bob’s post measurement states upon Alice measuring
\rho_{T\tilde{T}}^{\sigma}
. For each
a_{1}\in A_{1}
,
q_{1}\in Q_{1}
define
F_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})
such that
\alpha_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})=\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({F_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\tilde{T}}}\right)\rho_{T\tilde{T}}^{\sigma}}\right]
. Then, since any two purifications originating from
\sigma_{\tilde{T}}
are equivalent up to isometries on
\mathcal{H}_{T}
, there exists a unitary
U_{T}\in\mathcal{B}\left({\mathcal{H}_{T}}\right)
such that

(219)

\displaystyle\rho_{T\tilde{T}}^{\sigma}=\left({U^{\dagger}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)\rho^{max}_{T\tilde{T}}\left({U_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)

with
\rho^{max}_{T\tilde{T}}
the (non-normalized) maximally entangled state. Define

\ket{\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}}_{\tilde{T}}:=\sum_{k=1}^{\text{rank}(\sigma_{\tilde{T}})}\ket{e_{k}}_{\tilde{T}}
with
\left\{{\ket{e_{k}}}\right\}_{k}
the eigenbasis to
\sigma_{\tilde{T}}
. Then,

(220)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho_{T\tilde{T}}^{\sigma}&=\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)\ket{\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}}_{T}\bra{\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}}_{\tilde{T}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)\end{split}

and

(221)

\displaystyle\ket{\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}}_{T}\bra{\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}}_{\tilde{T}}=\left({U^{\dagger}_{T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\tilde{T}}}\right)\rho_{T\tilde{T}}^{max}\left({U_{T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\tilde{T}}}\right).\,

Thus,

(222)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})&=\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({F_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\tilde{T}}}\right)\rho_{T\tilde{T}}^{\sigma}}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{eqn:proof_assemblages_construction_1}}}{{=}}\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({F_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\tilde{T}}}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)\ket{\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}}_{T}\bra{\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}}_{\tilde{T}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)}\right]\\
&=\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)\left({F_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\tilde{T}}}\right)\ket{\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}}_{T}\bra{\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}}_{\tilde{T}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{prop:transpose_trick}}}{{=}}\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\left({F_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})}\right)^{T}}\right)\ket{\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}}_{T}\bra{\mathcal{I}_{\sigma}}_{\tilde{T}}\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{eqn:proof_assemblages_construction_2}}}{{=}}\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\left({F_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})}\right)^{T}}\right)\left({U^{\dagger}_{T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\tilde{T}}}\right)\rho_{T\tilde{T}}^{max}\left({U_{T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\tilde{T}}}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)}\right]\\
&=\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({U_{T}^{\dagger}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}\left({F_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})}\right)^{T}}\right)\rho_{T\tilde{T}}^{max}\left({U_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{cycl.}}}{{=}}\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({U_{T}^{\dagger}U_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}\left({F_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})}\right)^{T}}\right)\rho_{T\tilde{T}}^{max}\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)}\right]\\
&=\mathrm{tr}_{T}\left[{\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}\left({F_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})}\right)^{T}}\right)\rho_{T\tilde{T}}^{max}\left({\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\right)}\right]\\
&=\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}\left({F_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})}\right)^{T}\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}\end{split}

with the transposition is w.r.t. the eigenbasis of
\sigma_{\tilde{T}}
. Simply algebraic manipulation yield the pretty-good measurements

(223)

\displaystyle F_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})=\left({\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}\alpha_{\tilde{T}}(a_{1}|q_{1})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}}\right)^{T}\hskip 14.22636pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{prop:transpose_trick}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\hskip 14.22636ptF_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})=\left({\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}\alpha_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}}\right)^{T},\,

where, since
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{\tilde{T}}\right\rvert
, we may trivially reinterpret the operators as acting on
T
instead of
\tilde{T}
. Since the transposition is w.r.t. the eigenbasis of
\sigma_{T}
, we have
\left({\sigma_{T}}\right)^{T}
and similarly
\left({\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}}\right)^{T}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}
. Thus,

(224)

\displaystyle\begin{split}F_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})=\left({\alpha_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}}\right)^{T}\left({\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}}\right)^{T}=\left({\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}}\right)\left({\alpha_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})}\right)^{T}\left({\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}}\right)\end{split}

Moreover, from
\sigma_{T},\alpha_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\succcurlyeq 0
and the mappings
\left({\cdot}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and
(\cdot)^{T}
preserve positive semi-definiteness, it follows that
F_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})\succcurlyeq 0
. As the transposition map
(\cdot)^{T}
is linear and the index set
A_{1}
is finite, the
(\cdot)^{T}
-operation commutes with the summation over
a_{1}\in A_{1}
. By construction,
\sigma_{T}
is independent of
a_{1}\in A_{1}
. Thus, for all
q_{1}\in Q_{1}

(225)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{a_{1}}F_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}\sum_{a_{1}}\left({\alpha_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})}\right)^{T}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}\left({\sum_{a_{1}}\alpha_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})}\right)^{T}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{T}}}\left({\sigma_{T}}\right)^{T}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{\tilde{T}}}}=\mathcal{I}_{T}.\,\end{split}

In summary,

(226)

\displaystyle F_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}:=\sum_{a_{1},q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{a_{1}q_{1}}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}}\otimes F_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})

is feasible point to
\left({\mathrm{I}}\right)
. Since

(227)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(V_{A_{1}A_{2}Q_{1}Q_{2}}\otimes S_{\hat{T}\tilde{T}}\right)\left(\alpha_{A_{1}Q_{1}\tilde{T}}\otimes D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)\right]&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{prop:swap_trick_renner}}}{{=}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(V_{A_{1}A_{2}Q_{1}Q_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}\right)\left(\alpha_{A_{1}Q_{1}\hat{T}}D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)\right]\\
&=\mathrm{tr}\left[\left(V_{A_{1}A_{2}Q_{1}Q_{2}}\otimes\rho_{T\tilde{T}}^{\sigma}\right)\left(F_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes D_{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)\right]\end{split}

the construction maps feasible points of
\left({\mathrm{II}}\right)
to feasible points of
\left({\mathrm{I}}\right)
while preserving the objective value, and thus preserves optimality.
∎

Proof of
Lem. 3.1
.

Denote the problem in
Eq. 32
from
Lem. 3.1
as
\left({\mathrm{I}}\right)
and the problem in
Eq. 211
by
\left({\mathrm{II}}\right)
. We prove the equivalence between
\left({\mathrm{I}}\right)
and
\left({\mathrm{II}}\right)
. Recall,
\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{\hat{T}}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{\tilde{T}}\right\rvert
. The analysis may be restricted to the effect of the constraint that differs. Let
\beta_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}=\sum_{a_{1},q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{a_{1}q_{1}}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}}\otimes\beta_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})
be a feasible point to
\left({\mathrm{II}}\right)
. Hence,

(228)

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{1}}\beta_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})=\sum_{a_{1}}\beta_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1}^{\prime}),\,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall q_{1}^{\prime}\in Q_{1}

implies

(229)

\displaystyle\beta_{T}=\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\sum_{a_{1}}\beta_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})=\sum_{a_{1}}\beta_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1}^{\prime}),\,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall q_{1}^{\prime}\in Q_{1}

since
\sum_{a_{1}}\beta_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})
is independent of
q_{1}
for all
q_{1}\in Q_{1}
. Thus,
\beta_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}
is also a feasible point to
\left({\mathrm{I}}\right)
attaining the same objective value. As this holds for all feasible points, optimality is preserved. Conversely,
let
\gamma_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}=\sum_{a_{1},q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}\otimes\gamma_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})
be a feasible point to
\left({\mathrm{I}}\right)
. We claim

(230)

\displaystyle\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\sum_{a_{1}}\gamma_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})=\sum_{a_{1}}\gamma_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1}^{\prime}),\,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall q_{1}^{\prime}\in Q_{1}

implies

(231)

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{1}}\gamma_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})=\sum_{a_{1}}\gamma_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1}^{\prime}),\,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall q_{1}^{\prime}\in Q_{1}.\,

For each
q_{1}\in Q_{1}
define
F(q_{1}):=\sum_{a_{1}}\gamma_{T}(a_{1}|q_{1})
. Towards a contradiction, assume that there exist
q_{1},q_{1}^{\prime}\in Q_{1}
such that
F(q_{1})\neq F(q_{1}^{\prime})
. By the hypothesis,

(232)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}(F):=\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})F(q_{1})=F(q_{1}),\,\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}(F)=F(q_{1}^{\prime}).\,

Then,

(233)

\displaystyle F(q_{1})-F(q_{1}^{\prime})=\left({\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}(F)-F(q_{1})}\right)-\left({\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{1}}(F)-F(q_{1}^{\prime})}\right)=0

implying
F(q_{1})=F(q_{1}^{\prime})
, contradicting our assumption. Thus,
\gamma_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}
is feasible in
\left({\mathrm{II}}\right)
with the same objective value. As this holds for all feasible points, optimality is preserved.
∎

Appendix C
Bose-symmetry via Schur-Weyl duality

Central to the complexity result in
Sec. 6
is the intricate interplay between two groups that act naturally on
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
, viewed as a subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
. Concretely, for Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, the corresponding tensor product space
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
naturally admits a representation of
S_{n}
and
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
7
7

7

Let
G
be any group with representation space
\mathcal{H}
. Since a representation is a group homomorphism from
G
to
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
,
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
admits
G
’s group structure.
. Concretely, let
S_{n}
act
8
8

8

Since
S_{n}
is finite, to each
\pi\in S_{n}
there exists a group antihomorphism to a unitary permutation matrix
U_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}(\pi)
corresponding to this action.
on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
from the right by permuting the tensor factors of any element
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}\in\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
, i.e. for
\pi\in S_{n}
,

(234)

\displaystyle\left({\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}}\right)\pi=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}v_{\pi(i)},

and let the Lie group
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
act on each tensor factor simultaneously from the left
9
9

9

The direction of the action is interchangeable
, i.e. for any
g\in\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
,

(235)

\displaystyle g\left({\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}}\right)=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}g\cdot v_{i}.\,

Importantly, these two actions commute, and thus, the algebras of
S_{n}
- and
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-equivariant maps from
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
to
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
are full mutual centralizers in
\text{End}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)
. Schur-Weyl duality formalizes the relationship between the commuting actions of the finite-dimensional group algebra
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
and the general linear group
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
, demonstrating how these actions induce a decomposition of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
into simple modules. This duality establishes a correspondence between the simple
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-modules and the simple
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-modules within
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
, providing a powerful algebraic framework for analyzing the structure of the symmetric subspace. See
for an application of Schur-Weyl duality. Next, we introduce the
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-submodules in
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
via Weyl’s tensorial construction and connect them to the symmetric subspace.

Consider the decomposition of the complex group algebra
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
as a
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module (regular representation) given by Wedderburn’s little theorem

(236)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\simeq_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\bigoplus_{\lambda\vdash n}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}\right)}

where for each
\lambda
,
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\cdot c_{\lambda}
with Young symmetrizer
c_{\lambda}
is a simple
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module called Specht module. By Wedderburn’s little theorem, each isotypical component
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}\right)}
is the image of a primitive idempotent from the center of the group algebra
Z\left({\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\right)
. Note, while in general Young symmetrizers are minimal (primitive) idempotents, they are usually not central. In general, only a linear combination of Young symmetrizers is again central. The primary advantage, however, lies in the ability to explicitly construct a Young symmetrizer using a standard labeling of Young shape
Y(\lambda)
.

Weyl’s tensorial construction extends to the case where the representation space is not the group algebra but rather the
n
-fold tensor power of a vector space — or, in our context, a Hilbert space, i.e. ,
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
.
Since
c_{(n)}\in Z\left({\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\right)
is proportional to
P_{\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}
, we have
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)=\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\cdot c_{(n)}
. In general we define
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}:=\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\cdot c_{\lambda}
together with a Schur functor
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\left({\cdot}\right)
on the category of finite dimensional vector spaces. The functor takes
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
in the sense that it takes elements from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
and morphisms (
\mathbb{C}
-linear maps) on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
to morphisms on
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
.

Remark C.1
.

On a technical level, the Schur functor of partition
\lambda

(237)

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\,:\,\text{FinVect}\rightarrow\text{FinVect}

maps the category of finite dimensional vector spaces and linear maps thereon to itself. If
f,g\,:\,V\rightarrow W
are linear maps between finite dimensional vector spaces
V,W
, we define

(238)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}(f)\,:\,&\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}V\rightarrow\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}W,\,\\
\end{split}

with
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}(f\circ g)=\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}(f)\circ\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}(g)
and
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\left({\mathcal{I}_{V}}\right)=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}V}
. Strictly speaking the functor
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\left({\cdot}\right)
is distinct from its image space
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
which we refer to as a Weyl module to the reductive group
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\simeq\text{GL}_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}(\mathbb{C})
. See
[
FH04
, Sec. 6]
and
[
Mac95
, Appendix A]
for a more in depth discussion on polynomial functors.

Importantly, the Schur functors preserve the
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-module structure of
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
. Next, we will see how the Weyl modules relate to simple
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-modules.

Proposition C.2
.

We have

(239)

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}\simeq\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda},\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right).

Proof.

We have

(240)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}&=\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\cdot c_{\lambda}\simeq\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}c_{\lambda}=\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]c_{\lambda}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\cdot c_{\lambda}}}{{=}}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}^{*}\simeq\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}}{{\simeq}}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\simeq\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda},\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right).\,\end{split}

In the first line we used the definition of the
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-balanced product, i.e. the tensor product between right
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
and left
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
(see
Rem. F.12
and
Prop. F.43
). The second line follows from the property of the
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-balanced product to move
c_{\lambda}
from the second into the first
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module tensor factor. The last line follows from the definition of the contragradient (dual)
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}^{*}=\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda},\mathbb{C}}\right)
isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
.
∎

Thus, the symmetric subspace is the space of
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-invariant maps from the trivial Specht module
\mathcal{H}_{(n)}
to
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
. By Schur’s lemma, any such map is either an isomorphism between
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-modules or the zero map. Thus,
\mathbb{S}_{(n)}\mathcal{H}
is the space of isomorphisms between trivial Specht modules in
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
and
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathbb{S}_{(n)}\mathcal{H}}\right)
is the number of times (multiplicity)
\mathcal{H}_{(n)}
appears in
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
.

Remark C.3
.

While the Young symmetrizers are primitive idempotents in
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
, i.e. meaning they yield simple
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-modules, this property does not hold in
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
.

As the
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-action commutes with the
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
action on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
, the image of
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
in
\text{End}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)
is in
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)
. Furthermore, the image of any element of
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
is in
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)
if and only if it is in
Z\left({\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\right)
. We present the following proposition, which establishes Weyl modules—particularly the symmetric subspace—as submodules of the space of
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-invariant endomorphisms.

Proposition C.4

(
[
FH04
]
, Lemma 6.23)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
a finite dimensional complex vector space. The algebra
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)
is spanned as a linear subspace of
\text{End}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)
by
\text{End}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
. A subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
is a sub-
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)
-module if and only if it is invariant by
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
, i.e. a
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-submodule.

The characters of
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-module
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
to
g\in\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
with eigenvalues
x_{1}\,\ldots,x_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}
are

(241)

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}}(g)=S_{\lambda}\left({x_{1}\,\ldots,x_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}}\right)

where
S_{\lambda}\left({x_{1},\ldots,x_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}}\right)
are Schur polynomials (see e.g.
[
Mac95
]
) of degree
n
in
d_{\mathcal{H}}
variables. Concretely, for the symmetric subspace

(242)

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathbb{S}_{(n)}\mathcal{H}}(g)=H_{n}\left({x_{1},\ldots,x_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}}\right)

where
H_{n}\left({x_{1},\ldots,x_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}}\right)
are complete symmetric polynomials (see e.g.
[
FH04
, Appendix A]
for a relation between these polynomials). The Schur polynomials can be given in terms of semistandard Young tableaux

(243)

\displaystyle S_{\lambda}\left({x_{1},\ldots,x_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}}\right)=\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,\,d_{\mathcal{H}}}}x_{1}^{t_{1}}\cdot\cdot\cdot x_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}^{t_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}}=\sum_{\mu}K_{\lambda\mu}m_{\mu}

where the sum is indexed in semistandard labelings of Young shape
\lambda
with weight
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{d_{\mathcal{H}}})
. Alternatively, Kostka numbers
K_{\lambda\mu}
quantify how many semistandard labelings of Young shape
Y(\lambda)
of weight
\mu
exist and
m_{\mu}
are monomial symmetric functions. While a general closed form formula for
K_{\lambda\mu}
does not exist, for
\lambda=(n)
and
\mu\in\mathcal{T}_{d_{\mathcal{H}},n}
we have
K_{\lambda\mu}=1
.
In general,

(244)

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}}\right)=S_{\lambda}(1,\ldots,1)=\prod_{(i,j)\in Y(\lambda)}\frac{d_{\mathcal{H}}-i+j}{h_{\lambda}(i,j)}

with hook length
h_{\lambda}(i,j)
, i.e. number of cells in the Hook
H_{\lambda}(i,j)
. Then, by
[
FH04
, Theorem 6.3]
we recovers the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space dimension of the symmetric subspace given by
[
Har13
]
in

(245)

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)=H_{(n)}\left({1,\ldots,1}\right)=\binom{d_{\mathcal{H}}+n-1}{n}.\,

Furthermore, the characters certify that some
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
are zero, if the dimension of
\mathcal{H}
is small with respect to
n
.

Proposition C.5

(
[
FH04
]
Theorem 6.3 )
.

Let
k=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
. Then,
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
is zero if
\lambda_{k+1}\neq 0
.

The commutativity of the
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
- and
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-action also translate the simplicity of Specht modules to the Weyl modules. By
Prop. F.54
(ii), the simplicity of
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\cdot c_{\lambda}
implies the simplicity of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\cdot c_{\lambda}
as a
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)
-module. Furthermore, by
Prop. F.54
(i)

(246)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\cdot c_{\lambda}\simeq_{\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\cdot c_{\lambda}

and thus by
Prop. C.4
the spaces are also isomorphic as
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-modules. In summary, we have the following proposition.

Proposition C.6

(
[
FH04
]
, Theorem 6.3)
.

For each partition
\lambda\vdash_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}n
, the Weyl module
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
is a simple
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-module.

Remark C.7
.

While in this setting, the
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
are simple
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-modules for every partition
\lambda
of height at most
d_{\mathcal{H}}
, there exist other (e.g. dual representations) simple
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-modules which cannot be given in terms of
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
.

Schur-Weyl duality provides the following decompositions of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
. For the reader’s convenience, we provide a concise proof.

Proposition C.8

(Schur-Weyl Duality for
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\times\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
)
.

We have the decomposition of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
as a
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\times\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-module (i.e.
\left({\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right],\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
-bimodule)

(247)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\simeq\bigoplus_{\lambda\vdash_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}n}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}\end{split}

or as a
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-module

(248)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\simeq\bigoplus_{\lambda\vdash_{d_{\mathcal{H}}}n}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}}\right)^{\oplus m_{\lambda}}

Proof.

Follows directly from an application of the double centralizer theorem

(249)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}&\simeq\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{eqn:decomp_Sn_group_alebra}}}{{\simeq}}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\bigoplus_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}^{\oplus m_{\lambda}}\\
&\simeq\bigoplus_{\lambda}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}\right)^{\oplus m_{\lambda}}\simeq\bigoplus_{\lambda}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}}\right)^{\oplus m_{\lambda}}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{prop:V_decomp}}}{{\simeq}}\bigoplus_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}\otimes\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda},\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)\simeq\bigoplus_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}\otimes\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}.\,\end{split}

Note, that we used the semisimple decomposition of the regular
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module, and thus, direct sum in the exponent is over
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}\right)=m_{\lambda}
.
∎

Remark C.9
.

Recall,
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}V=0
for
\lambda
a partition of
n
into height greater than
\dim V
. In other words, the direct sum in
Eq. 247
and
Eq. 248
runs through all partitions
\lambda
of
n
with height at most
\dim V
.

Thus, considered as a
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-module,
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
decomposes into a direct sum of Weyl modules with multiplicity given by the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space dimension of the corresponding Specht module. Importantly, the symmetric subspace is one component in this decomposition. As an example consider
.

We are interested in operators with support and range on the symmetric subspace, i.e. elements from
\text{End}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
.

Proposition C.10
.

We have

(250)

\displaystyle\text{End}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)\simeq\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)

and

(251)

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\text{End}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)}\right)=\binom{d_{\mathcal{H}}+n-1}{n}^{2}\leq\left({n+1}\right)^{2d_{\mathcal{H}}}.\,

Proof.

The isomorphism follows from Schur-Weyl duality. Trivially,

(252)

\displaystyle\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)\subseteq\text{End}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right).\,

By the double-centralizer theorem in
Prop. F.53
,
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
and
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
form a double-commutant pair. The symmetric subspace
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
is a simple
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-module, and thus, by Schur’s lemma
\text{End}_{\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
is one-dimensional over
\mathbb{C}
. The only
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-equivariant endomorphisms of
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
are multiples of the identity map on
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
. Any endomorphism on
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
commutes with the
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-action as it is a simple
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-module. Thus, by Schur-Weyl duality, it lies in the image of
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
. Moreover, since
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
is defined by invariance under
S_{n}
-action, it follows that every endomorphism of
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
is also
S_{n}
-invariant. Thus,

(253)

\displaystyle\text{End}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)\subseteq\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right).\,

Furthermore, the
\mathbb{C}
vector space dimension follows from the character together with the definition of the symmetric subspace.
∎

Remark C.11
.

Note that, when considered as a vector space, the symmetric subspace of

\text{End}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)
– i.e.
\vee^{n}\left({\text{End}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)}\right)
– is distinct from
\text{End}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
.

C.1.
Full Matrix
*
-Algebra Structure of Bose-Symmetric States

We prove that the space of Bose-symmetric operators is isomorphic, as an algebra, to a complete matrix
*
-algebra of polynomial size. We then demonstrate the explicit construction of this isomorphism. Although the subsequent concepts are well established in the mathematical literature, we include the following material for the convenience of readers with a background in quantum information.

We require the decomposition of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
as
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
,
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
and
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\times\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-modules. For the reader’s convenience, relevant mathematical background is provided in
App. F
.

While the decomposition of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
as an
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-module yields the symmetric subspace component, in order to consider
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
as a full matrix algebra, we need to consider its decomposition as a
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module. Thus, we introduce an additional step in the decomposition of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
to argue on the computational complexity of
SDP_{n}^{Bose}(G)
.

It is a known classical result, that any unital matrix
*
-algebra is isomorphic to a direct sum of full matrix algebras. In what follows, we concretize this result in our specific setting and subsequently construct an explicit
*
-isomorphism.

Proposition C.12
.

The space
\mathcal{B}:=\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
is a unital matrix
*
-algebra.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{H}
is of finite dimension
d_{\mathcal{H}}
,
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathcal{H}}^{n}\times d_{\mathcal{H}}^{n}}
. Clearly, by linearity
\mathcal{B}
is closed under addition and scalar multiplication.
For
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{B}
,
v\in\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
any any
\sigma\in S_{n}
we have

(254)

\displaystyle\left({T_{1}T_{2}}\right)\left({\sigma\cdot v}\right)=T_{1}\left({T_{2}\left({\sigma\cdot v}\right)}\right)=T_{1}\left({\sigma\cdot T_{2}(v)}\right)=\sigma\cdot T_{1}\left({T_{2}(v)}\right)=\sigma\cdot\left({T_{1}T_{2}}\right)(v)

extending to
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
via linearity. Thus, given a basis of
\mathcal{B}
it is closed under matrix multiplication. Since
S_{n}
is finite its group action is unitary and thus

(255)

\displaystyle\left\langle\sigma\cdot v,T^{*}w\right\rangle=\left\langle T\left({\sigma\cdot v}\right),w\right\rangle=\left\langle T(v),\sigma^{-1}\cdot w\right\rangle=\left\langle v,T^{*}\left({\sigma^{-1}\cdot w}\right)\right\rangle

implying
T^{*}\left({\sigma\cdot v}\right)=\sigma\cdot T^{*}(v)
. If viewed as an embedding into
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
, the identity operator is given by the corresponding orthogonal projector onto
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
.
∎

Remark C.13
.

For finite dimensional
\mathcal{H}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{d_{\mathcal{H}}}
, the canonical inner product
\langle x,y\rangle=y^{\dagger}x
is
S_{n}
invariant. More generally, for a finite group
G
on
\mathbb{C}^{Z}
where
Z
is a set of finite cardinality, any representation

(256)

\displaystyle\begin{split}R\,:\,&G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{Z}\\
&g\mapsto R_{g},\,\end{split}

is unitary
[
Eti+11
, Theorem 3.11]
and thus,

(257)

\displaystyle g\cdot\left\langle v,w\right\rangle=\left\langle R_{g}v,R_{g}w\right\rangle=\left({R_{g}v}\right)^{\dagger}R_{g}w=w^{\dagger}R_{g}^{\dagger}R_{g}v=w^{\dagger}v=\left\langle v,w\right\rangle

proves the
G
-invariance of the inner product. Importantly,
\mathcal{B}
being closed under the conjugate transposed yields any orthoplement of a
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-submodule of
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
itself a
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-submodule.

Proposition C.14
.

The matrix
*
-algebra
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
is simple.

Proof.

It contains no non-trivial (proper) two-sided ideals. Equivalently, its center is given by the
\mathbb{C}
-span of the unit of
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
.
∎

Thus, it is isomorphic as an algebra to (a direct sum of) full matrix algebra(s).

Next, we exploit the fact that while the Weyl modules
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
are irreducible as
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-modules, they become reducible when viewed as
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-modules. By Maschke’s theorem,
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
is semisimple and thus any
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module decomposes into a direct sum of simple Specht modules
10
10

10

For fields of characteristic zero, i.e.
\mathbb{C}
, Specht modules are simple.
. Importantly, the symmetric subspace
\mathbb{S}_{(n)}\mathcal{H}
is isomorphic, as an
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module, to a direct sum of polynomial in
n
many isomorphic copies of the 1-dimensional trivial Specht module.

Proposition C.15
.

The symmetric subspace
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
is isomorphic as a
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module to a direct sum of
m_{(n)}=\binom{n+d_{\mathcal{H}}+1}{n}
many simple Specht modules isomorphic to the trivial Specht module
\mathcal{H}_{(n)}
, i.e.

(258)

\displaystyle\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\simeq_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\mathcal{H}_{(n)}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)}.\,

Proof.

By definition,
\sigma\cdot v=v
for any
v\in\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
and every
\sigma\in S_{n}
. Thus,
v
is an eigenvector with eigenvalue 1 under every group element of
S_{n}
. Hence, the restriction of the
S_{n}
-action on
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
is trivial: each
\sigma\in S_{n}
acts as the identity on
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
. Thus,
\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\left({v}\right)
is a simple
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module isomorphic to the trivial Specht module. The multiplicity corresponds to the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space dimension of
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
.
∎

In other words, the isotypic component of the trivial representation in
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
is precisely
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
. In general, we have the following proposition.

Proposition C.16
.

The Weyl modules in Weyl tensorial construction are isomorphic as
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-modules to a direct sum of isomorphic Specht modules

(259)

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}\right)}\simeq\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}}\right)}.\,

Proof.

With
Prop. F.36
we have

(260)

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}\right)}\simeq\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}\otimes\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda},\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)\simeq\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}}\right)}.\,

∎

By Schur’s Lemma,
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-invariant maps with support and range on
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
decompose accordingly.

Proposition C.17
.

We have

(261)

\displaystyle\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)\simeq\mathbb{C}^{m_{(n)}\times m_{(n)}}.\,

Proof.

In short, due to Schur’s lemma,
Hom
-space additivity and Wedderburn-Artin,

(262)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}\right)}}\right)&\simeq\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}\otimes\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda},\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right),\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}\right)}}\right)\\
&\simeq\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}}\right)},\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}\right)}}\right)\simeq\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}}\right)}}\right)\\
&\simeq M_{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}}\right)}\left({\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}}\right)}\right)\simeq M_{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}}\right)}\left({\mathbb{C}}\right).\,\end{split}

Specifically,

(263)

\displaystyle\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)\simeq M_{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathbb{S}_{(n)}\mathcal{H}}\right)}\left({\mathbb{C}}\right).\,

∎

Importantly, as
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
and
M_{\binom{n+d-1}{n}}\left({\mathbb{C}}\right)
are
*
-isomorphic as unital algebras, any such mapping preserves eigenvalues and thus positive semidefiniteness. The explicit construction of the
*
-isomorphism requires a decomposition of
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
as a
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module. Since
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
is invariant under the action of
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
, any subspace of
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
is similarly invariant. Consequently, we can identify
m_{(n)}
distinct subspaces, each of which is isomorphic as a
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module to the trivial Specht module
\mathcal{H}_{(n)}
. Let
U_{(n)}\subset\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
be isomorphic as a
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module to the trivial Specht module. Let
e_{(n)}\in U_{(n)}\backslash\left\{{0}\right\}
a non-zero vector in this module. Importantly, since
U_{(n)}
is simple,
U_{(n)}\simeq\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\cdot e_{(n)}
by cyclicity. Furthermore, consider the image of
e_{(n)}
under
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
, i.e.

(264)

\displaystyle W_{(n)}:=\left\{{Ae_{(n)}\,|\,A\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)}\right\},\,

with an orthonormal basis
B_{(n)}
. We have the following corollary of
[
Gij09
, Theorem 3]
.

Proposition C.18
.

The map

(265)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi\,:\,\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)&\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{m_{(n)}\times m_{(n)}}\\
A&\mapsto\left({\left\langle Ab,b^{\prime}\right\rangle_{S_{n}}}\right)_{b,b^{\prime}\in B_{(n)}}\end{split}

is an algebra
*
-isomorphism.

Each
A\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
is thus mapped to its restriction on
W_{(n)}
which in turn is the image of
e_{(n)}
under the action of
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
. Thus, the
*
-isomorphism can be made explicit given a non-zero vector in
U_{(n)}
and an explicit computable
11
11

11

Since
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
as an algebra is closed under multiplication,
AA^{\prime}
is again in
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
.

\left\langle AA^{\prime}e_{(n)},A^{\prime\prime}e_{(n)}\right\rangle
for any
A,A^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime}
from a basis to
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
. Since isomorphic
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-modules are connected by a
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-invariant map, i.e. an element from
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
, the expressions
A^{\prime}e_{(n)}
and
A^{\prime\prime}e_{(n)}
corresponds to
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-modules isomorphic to
U_{(n)}
.

Proposition C.19
.

We have
W_{(n)}\simeq\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
.

Proof.

Recall,

(266)

\displaystyle\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m_{(n)}}V_{i},\,

where
U_{(n)}\simeq V_{i}\simeq V_{j}
for
i,j=1,\ldots,m_{(n)}
are isomorphic as
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-modules. For each
i=1,\ldots,m_{(n)}
, let
v_{i}
be a non-zero basis vector spanning
V_{i}
. Thus,
\left\{{v_{i}}\right\}_{i=1}^{m_{(n)}}
is a direct sum basis for
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
. From
Prop. C.10
any linear operator on
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
is Bose-symmetric, and hence, the full matrix algebra
M_{m_{(n)}}(\mathbb{C})
indexed in
\left\{{v_{i}}\right\}_{i=1}^{m_{(n)}}
basis is Bose-symmetric. Let
w\in\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
be any vector which can be uniquely written as

(267)

\displaystyle w=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{(n)}}\alpha_{i}v_{i}.\,

Define
A
as the
\mathbb{C}
-linear map determined by

(268)

\displaystyle A(v_{1})=w,\,\hskip 28.45274ptA(v_{j})=0\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for }j=2,\ldots,m_{(n)}.\,

Let
\left[{A}\right]
denote the matrix representation of
A
in the
\left\{{v_{i}}\right\}_{i=1}^{m_{(n)}}
basis. Then,

(269)

\displaystyle\left[{A}\right]_{i,1}=\alpha_{i},\,\hskip 28.45274ptA_{i,j}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for }i=1,\ldots,m_{(n)},\,j=2,\ldots,m_{n}.\,

Alternatively, let
v_{1}^{*}
be the dual vector to
v_{1}
, then
A=wv_{1}^{*}
.
Since
S_{n}
acts trivially on any element of the symmetric subspace
A
is Bose-symmetric. Thus,
v_{1}
can be mapped to any
w\in\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
under some intertwiner
A
. Due to the isomorphism, any other
v_{j}
that is mapped to
w
under the intertwiner yields the same outcome.
∎

With the proposition above, we can provide an explicit
*
-isomorphism given a basis to the symmetric subspace. In the previous section, we provided such a basis following the approach of
[
Har13
]
. Here, we provide a more detailed derivation of this basis using highest weight theory and semistandard Young tableaux. In doing so, we establish a connection between our approach where
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
is decomposed as a
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-module and
[
Chr+07
]
, where
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
was studied as a
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
-module and Bose-symmetric states were constructed from a highest weight vector.

We can decompose each Weyl module, a highest weight module, into its weight spaces, i.e. a collection of vectors with the same weight. The action of the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
of
\mathfrak{gl}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
, i.e. the Lie algebra to the maximal torus of
\text{GL}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
, makes this concrete. Consider the tensor power action of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathcal{H})
on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
. Then, the weight module admits a decomposition

(270)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}}\underbrace{\left\{{w\in\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}\,|\,H\cdot w=\mu w,\,H\in\mathfrak{H}}\right\}}_{=:\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)_{\mu}}

where each
\mu
arises as a sum over the tensor factors of the weights of
\mathfrak{h}
and
H\in\mathfrak{h}
with
H=\text{diag}\left({h_{1},\ldots,h_{d_{h}}}\right)
acts on simple tensors in the canonical basis to
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
as

(271)

\displaystyle H\cdot\left({\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}e_{i_{j}}}\right)=\left({\prod_{j=1}^{n}h_{i_{j}}}\right)\left({\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}e_{i_{j}}}\right).\,

and thus on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
extended by linearity. Restricting the action of
\mathfrak{h}
to a linear subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
we get an according weight space decomposition of the symmetric subspace

(272)

\displaystyle\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\simeq\bigoplus_{\mu\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}}\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\cap\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)_{\mu}.\,

It is easy to see that the intersection of the linear subspaces is a again a linear subspace. While their exists potentially many elements of the canonical basis to
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
with the same weight, under the idempotent
c_{(n)}
projecting onto
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
, they become a single vector in
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
. In other words, the collection of vectors with the same weight become the same symmetrized vector in
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
, i.e.

(273)

\displaystyle\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\cap\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)_{\mu}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)_{\mu}\cdot c_{(n)}}\right).\,

Since
\mathfrak{h}
is abelian, the weight is a class function for types. As a tuple
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
where
\alpha_{j}
counts how many times the canonical basis vector
e_{j}
appears in the simple tensor representing a canonical basis element of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
. Thus, the weight spaces in
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
can be identified with semistandard labellings of Young shape
Y((n))
with entries from
\left[{d_{\mathcal{H}}}\right]
. The dimension of the weight spaces is given by the number of semistandard tableaux there are with the corresponding weight content.

Proposition C.20
.

For each
\mu
,
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\cap\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)_{\mu}
the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space dimension is one.

Proof.

For Young shape
Y((n))
there exists exactly one semistandard labeling encoding a given weight.
∎

Proposition C.21
.

Let
\tau
be a semistandard labeling with weight
\mu
of Young shape
Y((n))
. Consider the image of the corresponding Young symmetrizer in
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
(polytabloid), i.e.

(274)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{H}}^{n}}\ni u_{\tau}=\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\sim\tau}\bigotimes_{y\in Y((n))}e_{\tau^{\prime}(y)}.\,

where
\tau^{\prime}\sim\tau
is any tableau which lies in the orbit of
\tau
under the row stabilizer subgroup of
\tau
. Then,

(275)

\displaystyle\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\cap\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)_{\mu}\simeq\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\left({u_{\tau}}\right)\simeq\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\cdot u_{\tau}.\,

Furthermore, the weight spaces in
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
are isomorphic as
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-modules to the trivial Specht module.

Proof.

Since
u_{\tau}
has weight
\mu
it follows that
u_{\tau}\in\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\cap\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)_{\mu}
. As
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)\cap\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)_{\mu}
has
\mathbb{C}
-vector space dimension one, the first vector space isomorphism in
Eq. 275
follows. By construction, for any
v\in\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
,
S_{n}\cdot v=v
. Thus,
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
acts by
\mathbb{C}
-scalar multiplication on
u_{\tau}
, i.e.
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\cdot u_{\tau}=\text{span}_{\mathbb{C}}\left({u_{\tau}}\right)
and the second isomorphism follows. As
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-modules, the spaces are isomorphic to the trivial Specht module.
∎

Proposition C.22
.

The set
\left\{{u_{\tau}\,:\,\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{(n),d_{\mathcal{H}}}}\right\}
is a basis of the symmetric subspace and a representative set for the action of
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
on
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
.

Proof.

By the arguments above, the set spans
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
and is of cardinality
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
. The set satisfies the conditions in
Prop. F.44
, and thus, is a representative for
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
on
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
.
∎

Remark C.23
.

For partition
\lambda=(n)
, there exists a bijection between the set
\mathcal{T}_{(n),d_{\mathcal{H}}}
of semi-standard labelings with entries from
\left[{k}\right]
of Young shape
Y\left({(n)}\right)
and the set
\text{Par}\left({d_{\mathcal{H}},n}\right)
of partitions of
n
into at most
d_{\mathcal{H}}
parts. Thus, the connection to
\left\{{\ket{s_{\vec{t}}}\,:\,\vec{t}\in\text{Par}\left({d_{\mathcal{H}},n}\right)}\right\}
from
[
Har13
]
is clear. For more complex partitions this bijection no longer holds.

We are now prepared to explicitly construct a
*
-isomorphism between the space of Bose-symmetric operators and a full matrix algebra over
\mathbb{C}
, whose size scales polynomial in
n
.

Proposition C.24
.

The map

(276)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi\,:\,\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)&\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{m_{(n)}\times m_{(n)}}\\
A&\mapsto\left({\left\langle Au_{\tau},u_{\gamma}\right\rangle_{S_{n}}}\right)_{\tau,\gamma\in\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,d}}\end{split}

is a bijection preserving semi-definiteness. Moreover, there exist a unitary matrix
U_{(n)}
such that
U_{(n)}^{*}AU_{(n)}=\psi(A)
, for all
A\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\right)
.

Proof.

Follows directly from the arguments above. The change of basis matrix
U_{(n)}
is constructed from column vectors
u_{\tau}
with
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{n,d_{\mathcal{H}}}
from
Prop. C.22
.
∎

Remark C.25
.

Since
U_{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{H}}^{n}\times m_{(n)}}
,
U^{*}=U^{T}
. Since

(277)

\displaystyle\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)=\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{n,d_{\mathcal{H}}}}\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]\cdot u_{\tau}

U_{(n)}
is also referred to as an element of a representative matrix set (see
[
Pol20
, Definition 2.4.5]
) for the
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-action on
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
. In other words, it contains a collection of non-zero vectors from
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
which generate
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
upon
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
action. While in the case of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
, the representative matrix set has a higher cardinality (corresponding to the number of
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-isotypic components in
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
), for
\vee^{n}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
, it consists solely of
U_{(n)}
.

Remark C.26
.

Similarly, we can obtain a
*
-isomorphism for any Weyl module
\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}
, i.e.

(278)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi\,:\,\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}^{\oplus\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({V_{\lambda}}\right)}}\right)&\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{m_{\lambda}\times m_{\lambda}}\\
A&\mapsto\left({\left\langle Au_{\tau},u_{\gamma}\right\rangle_{S_{n}}}\right)_{\tau,\gamma\in\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,d}}\end{split}

with
m_{\lambda}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathbb{S}_{\lambda}\mathcal{H}}\right)
and
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,d}
the set of semistandard labeligns of Young shape
Y(\lambda)
with entries from
\left[{d_{\mathcal{H}}}\right]
.

Appendix D
Proof of
Lem. 7.5

Proof.

We explicitly express
\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}
in terms of its standard basis to
\mathcal{B}\left({A_{1}Q_{1}T}\right)
, i.e.

(279)

\displaystyle\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[\left\lvert{A_{1}}\right\rvert\right]\,,\\
q_{1}\in\left[\left\lvert{Q_{1}}\right\rvert\right]\end{subarray}}\ket{a_{1}q_{1}}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\otimes\sum_{i,j\in\left[\lvert T\rvert\right]}\ket{i}\bra{j}_{T}\otimes\rho^{a_{1},q_{1},i,j}_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\,.

Thus,

(280)

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}_{A_{1}}\left[{\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}}\right]=\sum_{q_{1}\in\left[\left\lvert{Q_{1}}\right\rvert\right]}\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\sum_{i,j\in\left[\lvert T\rvert\right]}\ket{i}\bra{j}_{T}\otimes\sum_{a_{1}\in\left[{\left\lvert{A_{1}}\right\rvert}\right]}\rho^{a_{1},q_{1},i,j}_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}

Since
\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({(A_{1}Q_{1}T)\otimes\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)
, i.e.

(281)

\displaystyle\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[\left\lvert{A_{1}}\right\rvert\right]\,,\\
q_{1}\in\left[\left\lvert{Q_{1}}\right\rvert\right]\end{subarray}}\ket{a_{1}q_{1}}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\otimes\sum_{i,j\in\left[\lvert T\rvert\right]}\ket{i}\bra{j}_{T}\otimes U_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\,(\pi)\rho^{a_{1},q_{1},i,j}_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\,U^{*}_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}(\pi)

where
U_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}(\pi)
corresponds to a permutation matrix representation for any
\pi\in S_{n}
we deduce that for any
\pi\in S_{n}

(282)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathrm{tr}_{A_{1}}\left[{\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref {eqn:sym_ext_state}}}{{=}}\\
