\Rightarrow\hskip 14.22636pt&\mathrm{tr}_{A_{1}}\left[{\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}}\right]\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({(Q_{1}T)\otimes(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\right)\,.\end{split}

Now for the corresponding RHS of the constraint in

we have

(283)

\displaystyle\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{(A_{1}Q_{1})}\left[\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\right]\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({(Q_{1}T)\otimes(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\right)

by a similar argument concluding the proof of

\displaystyle\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}=\sum_{i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\right\rvert}\right]}\ket{i}\bra{j}_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{2}\in\left[{\left\lvert{A_{2}}\right\rvert}\right]\,,\\

in the standard basis to the respective spaces. From the

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}&=\left({\mathcal{I}_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}}\otimes U_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{2}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\\

\forall\pi\in S_{n-1}
. Hence, for any

(287)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathrm{tr}_{(A_{2})_{1}}\left[{\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}}\right]\\

&\hskip 42.67912pt\otimes\sum_{a_{2}\in\left[{\left\lvert{(A_{2})_{1}}\right\rvert}\right]}U_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{2}^{n}}\rho^{\left({i,j,a_{2},q_{2},u,v}\right)}_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{2}^{n}}U^{*}_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{2}^{n}}\\

&\left({\mathrm{tr}_{(A_{2})_{1}}\left[{\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}}\right]}\right)\left({\mathcal{I}_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{(Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}}\otimes U^{*}_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{2}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\\
\Rightarrow\,&\mathrm{tr}_{(A_{2})_{1}}\left[{\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}}\right]\,\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n-1}}\right]\,}\left({(A_{1}Q_{1}T)\otimes(Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}\otimes(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{2}^{n}}\right)\\
\Rightarrow\,&\mathrm{tr}_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2})_{1}}\left[{\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}}\right]\,\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n-1}}\right]\,}\left({(Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}\otimes(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{2}^{n}}\right)\,.\end{split}

For the corresponding RHS, we have

(288)

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{q_{2}}\pi_{2}(q_{2})\ket{q_{2}}\bra{q_{2}}_{Q_{2}}\otimes\frac{\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}}{\lvert T\rvert}\right)\otimes\rho_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{2}^{n}}\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n-1}}\right]\,}\left({(Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}\otimes(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{2}^{n}}\right)

by a similar argument, thus, proofing
Eq. 151
. Lastly, the bound on the dimension follows directly from
Prop. 7.4
.
∎

Appendix E
Proof of
Lem. 7.9

Before we prove
Lem. 7.9
, we construct a canonical basis to
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{n}}\right)
from
S_{n}
orbits, where
\mathcal{A}
is some Hilbert space. See
Sec. 6
for an alternative construction following
[
Gij09
]
. See
for examples that elaborate on various concepts discussed in this section.

Notation

Since
\left({\mathbb{C}^{d}}\right)^{\otimes n}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{d\cdot n}
, we often switch between these description and only explicitly denote the ismorphism by
\phi(\cdot)
when needed.

Any
Z\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{n}}\right)
is invariant under the group action. Consequently, each corresponding basis element is also invariant. Averaging over the group yields an invariant operator. Therefore, given a basis element to
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{n}
, averaging over its group orbit produces an invariant vector. From these we can construct invariant operators. The smallest spanning set of invariant operators constructed via averaging over the orbits starting from each basis element to
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{n}
corresponds to an invariant basis to
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{n}}\right)
.

Concretely, we consider the orbits of the group action of
S_{n}
on pairs
12
12

12

Basis to a matrix algebra indexed in tuples.

(289)

\displaystyle\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{A}}\right\rvert\times\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert^{n}}\right]^{2}.\,

Let
i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{A}}\right\rvert\times\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert^{n}}\right]
denote an index of the standard basis to
\left({\mathcal{A}}\right)\times\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)^{n}
. Then,

(290)

\displaystyle O(i,j):=\left({\pi(i),\pi(j)\,:\,\pi\in S_{n}}\right)

with
\pi(i)=\left({\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes U_{(\mathcal{H})_{1}^{n}}(\pi)}\right)\ket{i}
, i.e. for
\ket{i}\in\mathcal{H}^{n}

(291)

\displaystyle\begin{split}U_{(\mathcal{H})_{1}^{n}}(\pi)\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\ket{\phi_{j}(i)}_{j}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\ket{\phi_{j}(i)}_{\pi^{-1}(j)}.\,\end{split}

Naturally, the action of
S_{n}
partitions the set in
Eq. 289
into

(292)

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\mathcal{A}}\right\rvert^{2}\binom{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert+n-1}{n}^{2}\leq m\left({\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)

number of orbits. We represent each orbit
O_{r}
indexed in
r\in m\left({\mathcal{A},\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert^{n}}\right)
as an adjacency matrix

(293)

\displaystyle C_{r}\in\left\{{0,1}\right\}^{\left({\left\lvert{\mathcal{A}}\right\rvert\times\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert^{n}}\right)\times\left({\left\lvert{\mathcal{A}}\right\rvert\times\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert^{n}}\right)},\,

where the matrix entries are given by

(294)

\displaystyle\left({C_{r}}\right)_{i,j}=\begin{cases}1\hskip 4.26773pt\text{ if }(i,j)\in O_{r}\\
0\hskip 4.26773pt\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Remark E.1
.

Given an element
(a,b)
we denote the corresponding orbit via
O_{(a,b)}
and the corresponding adjacency matrix by
C_{(a,b)}
.

Then,

(295)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\left({\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H}^{n}}\right):=\left\{{C_{i}}\right\}_{i=1}^{m\left({\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H}^{n}}\right)}

is a canonical basis to
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{n}}\right)
and

(296)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lvert{\mathcal{C}\left({\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H}^{n}}\right)}\right\rvert&=\dim\left({\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{n}}\right)}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{prop:bounding_our_case}}}{{\leq}}\left\lvert{\mathcal{A}}\right\rvert^{2}(n+1)^{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert^{2}}.\,\end{split}

Remark E.2
.

In each orbit, we can choose the representative element to be of the form

(297)

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{j=1}^{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\left[{\bigotimes_{i=\lambda_{j-1}+1}^{\lambda_{j}}\ket{j}_{i}}\right]\left[{\bigotimes_{i=\lambda_{j-1}+1}^{\lambda_{j}}\bra{j}_{i}}\right]

with
\lambda_{0}=0
and
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert})\vdash_{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}n
, i.e. in each system
i\in\left[{n}\right]
the basis element is given by
\ket{l}\bra{k}_{i}
for
l,k\in\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right]
. As the
S_{n}
-action permutes the system indices, this does not alter the number of systems described by basis element
\ket{l}\bra{k}_{i}
for
l,k\in\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right]
. The
S_{n}
-action conserves the described set partitioning.

Remark E.3
.

Note that with a canonical basis
\left\{{\ket{i}}\right\}_{i=1}^{\left\lvert{\mathcal{A}}\right\rvert}
to
\mathcal{A}
a canonical basis to
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)
is given by

(298)

\displaystyle\left\{{\ket{i}\bra{i}_{\mathcal{A}}\otimes C_{t}^{\mathcal{H}}}\right\}_{i\in\left\lvert{\mathcal{A}}\right\rvert,\,t\in m(\mathcal{H})}

where the
C_{t}^{\mathcal{H}}
are canonical basis elements to
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}}\right)
.

Thus, ignoring
\mathcal{A}
, a common procedure (see e.g.
[
CTV24
]
) to efficiently determine the representative elements of the
S_{n}
-orbits exploits a bijection between said orbits and certain matrices

(299)

\displaystyle\left\{{E_{i}}\right\}_{i}^{m\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert\times\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}_{\succcurlyeq 0}.\,

Concretely, for an arbitrary representative element
(x,y)
to orbit
O^{\mathcal{H}^{n}}_{i}
we construct the matrix

(300)

\displaystyle\left({E^{(x,y)}}\right)_{a,b}=\left\lvert{\left\{{k\in\left[{n}\right]\,:\,\phi_{k}(x)=a,\phi_{k}(y)=b}\right\}}\right\rvert,\,\text{ with }(a,b)\in\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right]\times\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right],\,

by determining the frequencies of basis elements
\ket{\phi_{i}(x)}\bra{\phi_{i}(y)}
to
\mathcal{B}\left({\mathcal{H}^{n}}\right)
appearing in
(x,y)\in O^{\mathcal{H}^{n}}_{i}
. Conversely, by solving

(301)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{array}[]{cc}\text{find}&\mathcal{E}:=\left\{{E^{(x,y)}}\right\}_{(x,y)\in\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert^{n}}\right]\times\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert^{n}}\right]}\\
\\
\text{s.t.}&\sum_{a,b}\left({E^{(x,y)}}\right)_{a,b}=n\\
&\left({E^{(x,y)}}\right)_{a,b}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\end{array}\end{split}

we can efficiently w.r.t.
n
determine a representative element for each orbit
O_{i}^{\mathcal{H}^{n}}
.

Remark E.4
.

Computing the full orbit would require at worst
\left[{S_{n}}\right]=n!
many permutations.

Proof of
Lem. 7.9
.

The proof is based on
[
CTV24
,
FST22
,
Pol20
,
LPS16
]
and is structured as follows:

First, we show that any
Z\in\text{End}^{S_{t\left({\mathcal{D}_{1}}\right)}}\left({\mathcal{D}_{1}}\right)
, and in particular any canonical basis element thereof, can be efficiently transformed into its block-diagonal form allowing for an efficient formulation of the positive semidefinite constraint. Then, we demonstrate that the other SDP constraints and the objective function can be efficiently formulated in terms of the canonical basis of the respective spaces. See
Sec. 6.3
for a similar proof strategy.

Since
Prop. 7.4
bounds the number of blocks to be growing polynomially in
n
, it suffices to show that each block in the decomposition given in
Eq. 161
can be computed efficiently w.r.t.
n
. Thus, for any
Z\in\text{End}^{S_{t\left({\mathcal{D}_{1}}\right)}}\left({\mathcal{D}_{1}}\right)
, the only costly basis transformation is

(302)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i=1}^{m\left({\mathcal{H}^{n}}\right)}z_{i}\cdot U^{T}_{\lambda}C_{i}^{\mathcal{H}}U_{\lambda},\,\end{split}

with
z_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
. The
U_{\lambda}
consist of column vectors
\left\{{u_{\tau}}\right\}_{\tau\in T_{\lambda,\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}}
indexed in the number of semistandard Young tableaus of shape
\lambda
indexed in
\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right]
and as these are bounded to only grow polynomially in
n
we can deduce the efficient computation of
Eq. 302
from an efficient computation of

(303)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m\left({\mathcal{H}^{n}}\right)}z_{i}\cdot u_{\tau}^{T}C_{i}^{\mathcal{H}}u_{\tau^{\prime}}

with
\tau,\tau^{\prime}\in T_{\lambda,\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}
. While clearly
u_{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert^{n}}
and
C_{i}^{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert^{n}\times\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert^{n}}
grow exponentially in
n
,
\forall\tau\in T_{\lambda,\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert},i\in\left[{m\left({\mathcal{H}^{n}}\right)}\right]
, we will argue that while we cannot efficiently compute these quantities, we can however efficiently compute
u_{\tau}^{T}C_{i}^{\mathcal{H}}u_{\tau^{\prime}}
, i.e. the entries in the transformed matrix
U^{T}_{\lambda}C_{i}^{\mathcal{H}}U_{\lambda}
. Concretely, by
Prop. E.5
below, given
\left\{{z_{i}}\right\}_{i\in m(\mathcal{H}^{n})}

Eq. 303
can be computed polynomially in
n
for fixed
\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert
.

While we have now shown that we can efficiently compute the bases to the set of full matrix algebras in the image of
\Psi_{\text{End}^{S_{t\left({\mathcal{D}_{1}}\right)}}(\mathcal{D}_{1})}(\cdot)
in
Eq. 157
, we have yet to show that each constraint in
Eq. 161
can be efficiently formulated in their respective canonical basis. Let

(304)

\displaystyle\left\{{\ket{a_{1}q_{1}i}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[{A_{1}}\right],\,\\
q_{1}\in\left[{Q_{1}}\right],\,\\
i\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right]\end{subarray}}

be the standard basis to
A_{1}Q_{1}T
and

(305)

\displaystyle\left\{{C_{j}^{\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right\}_{j\in\left[{m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)}\right]}

be the canonical basis to
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)
. Then, by
Rem. E.3
we can express
\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}\in\text{End}^{S_{t\left({\mathcal{D}_{1}}\right)}}(\mathcal{D}_{1})
in the corresponding basis

(306)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\left({\mathcal{D}_{1}}\right)}:=\left\{{\ket{a_{1}q_{1}i}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}j}_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes C_{t}^{\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[{A_{1}}\right],\,\\
q_{1}\in\left[{Q_{1}}\right],\,\\
i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
t\in\left[{m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)}\right]\end{subarray}},\,

i.e.

(307)

\displaystyle\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[{A_{1}}\right],\,\\
q_{1}\in\left[{Q_{1}}\right],\,\\
i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
t\in\left[{m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)}\right]\end{subarray}}v_{a_{1},a_{1},q_{1},q_{1},i,j,t}\cdot\ket{a_{1}q_{1}i}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}j}_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes C_{t}^{\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}},\,

with coefficients
v_{a_{1},a_{1},q_{1},q_{1},i,j,t}\in\mathbb{C}
. For ease of notation, let us define for every
a_{1}\in\left[{\left\lvert{A_{1}}\right\rvert}\right],q_{1}\in\left[{\left\lvert{Q_{1}}\right\rvert}\right],i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right],\,t\in\left[{m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)}\right]

(308)

\displaystyle Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t):=\ket{a_{1}q_{1}i}\bra{a_{1}q_{1}j}_{A_{1}Q_{1}T}\otimes C_{t}^{\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}},\,

such that

(309)

\displaystyle\rho_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}^{n}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[{A_{1}}\right],\,\\
q_{1}\in\left[{Q_{1}}\right],\,\\
i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
t\in\left[{m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)}\right]\end{subarray}}v_{a_{1},a_{1},q_{1},q_{1},i,j,t}\cdot Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t).\,

Then, we explicitly express
Eq. 161
in terms of these
Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t)
, i.e.

(310)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&SDP^{(n)}(T,V,\pi)=\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert\max_{v_{a_{1},a_{1},q_{1},q_{1},i,j,t}\in\mathbb{C}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[{A_{1}}\right],\,\\
q_{1}\in\left[{Q_{1}}\right],\,\\
i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
t\in\left[{m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)}\right]\end{subarray}}v_{a_{1},a_{1},q_{1},q_{1},i,j,t}\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\cdot\mathrm{tr}\left[{\left({V_{a_{1},a_{2},q_{1},q_{2}}\otimes S_{T\hat{T}}}\right)\mathrm{tr}_{(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{2}^{n}}\left[{Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t)}\right]}\right]\\
\\
\text{s.t.}\hskip 14.22636pt&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[{A_{1}}\right],\,\\
q_{1}\in\left[{Q_{1}}\right],\,\\
i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
t\in\left[{m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)}\right]\end{subarray}}v_{a_{1},a_{1},q_{1},q_{1},i,j,t}\cdot\left[\!\left[\Psi_{\text{End}^{S_{t\left({\mathcal{D}_{1}}\right)}}(\mathcal{D}_{1})}\left({Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t)}\right)\right]\!\right]_{\lambda}\succcurlyeq 0,\,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\lambda\in\text{Par}\left({\left\lvert{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right\rvert,n}\right),\,\\
&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[{A_{1}}\right],\,\\
q_{1}\in\left[{Q_{1}}\right],\,\\
i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
t\in\left[{m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)}\right]\end{subarray}}v_{a_{1},a_{1},q_{1},q_{1},i,j,t}\cdot\mathrm{tr}\left[Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t)\right]=1,\,\\
\\
&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[{A_{1}}\right],\,\\
q_{1}\in\left[{Q_{1}}\right],\,\\
i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
t\in\left[{m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)}\right]\end{subarray}}v_{a_{1},a_{1},q_{1},q_{1},i,j,t}\cdot\left[\!\left[\Psi_{\text{End}^{S_{t\left({\mathcal{D}_{2}}\right)}}(\mathcal{D}_{2})}\left({\mathrm{tr}_{A_{1}}\left[{Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t)}\right]}\right)\right]\!\right]_{\lambda}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[{A_{1}}\right],\,\\
q_{1}\in\left[{Q_{1}}\right],\,\\
i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
t\in\left[{m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)}\right]\end{subarray}}v_{a_{1},a_{1},q_{1},q_{1},i,j,t}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\cdot\left[\!\left[\Psi_{\text{End}^{S_{t\left({\mathcal{D}_{2}}\right)}}(\mathcal{D}_{2})}\left({\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\left[{Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t)}\right]}\right)\right]\!\right]_{\lambda},\,\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\forall\lambda\in\text{Par}\left({\left\lvert{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right\rvert,n}\right),\,\\
\\
&\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[{A_{1}}\right],\,\\
q_{1}\in\left[{Q_{1}}\right],\,\\
i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
t\in\left[{m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)}\right]\end{subarray}}v_{a_{1},a_{1},q_{1},q_{1},i,j,t}\cdot\left[\!\left[\Psi_{\text{End}^{S_{t\left({\mathcal{D}_{3}}\right)}}(\mathcal{D}_{3})}\left({\mathrm{tr}_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2})_{1}}\left[{Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t)}\right]}\right)\right]\!\right]_{\lambda}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in\left[{A_{1}}\right],\,\\
q_{1}\in\left[{Q_{1}}\right],\,\\
i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
t\in\left[{m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)}\right]\end{subarray}}v_{a_{1},a_{1},q_{1},q_{1},i,j,t}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\cdot\left[\!\left[\Psi_{\text{End}^{S_{t\left({\mathcal{D}_{3}}\right)}}(\mathcal{D}_{3})}\left({\sum_{q_{2}}\pi_{2}(q_{2})\ket{q_{2}}\bra{q_{2}}_{Q_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}}\left[{Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t)}\right]}\right)\right]\!\right]_{\lambda},\,\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\forall\lambda\in\text{Par}\left({\left\lvert{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right\rvert,n-1}\right).\,\\
\end{split}

We argued that any
Z\in\text{End}^{S_{t(D_{1})}}\left({D_{1}}\right)
can be efficiently transformed into block diagonal form. Thus, the positive semidefinete constraints are efficiently computable with respect to these bases. Subsequently, we demonstrate that the normalization constraint can also be computed efficiently. Next, we show that the operators (matrices) appearing in the constraints in
Eq. 310
can be efficiently expressed in the respective canonical basis. With the bound on
m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)}\right)
from
Prop. 7.4
, it suffices to restrict the proofs to a respective summand in the sums appearing in
Eq. 310
, i.e. we show, that for all indices

(311)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{tr}_{A_{1}}\left[{Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t)}\right]\,\land\,\sum_{q_{1}}\pi_{1}(q_{1})\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{A_{1}Q_{1}}\left[{Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t)}\right]\end{split}

can be efficiently expressed in terms of the canonical basis to
\text{End}^{S_{t\left({\mathcal{D}_{2}}\right)}}(\mathcal{D}_{2})

(312)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}:=\left\{{\ket{q_{1}}\bra{q_{1}}_{Q_{1}}\otimes\ket{i}\bra{j}_{T}\otimes C_{t}^{\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\in\left[{\left\lvert{Q_{1}}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
i,j\in\left[{\left\lvert{T}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
t\in m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{1}^{n}}\right)\end{subarray}}

and analogously,

(313)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{tr}_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2})_{1}}\left[{Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t)}\right]\,\land\,\sum_{q_{2}}\pi_{2}(q_{2})\ket{q_{2}}\bra{q_{2}}_{Q_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\hat{T}}\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{(A_{1}Q_{1}T)(A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T})_{1}}\left[{Z(a_{1},q_{1},i,j,t)}\right]\end{split}

in terms of the canonical basis to
\text{End}^{S_{t\left({\mathcal{D}_{3}}\right)}}(\mathcal{D}_{3})

(314)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{D}_{3}}:=\left\{{\ket{q_{2}}\bra{q_{2}}_{(Q_{2})_{1}}\otimes\ket{u}\bra{v}_{\hat{T}_{1}}\otimes K_{t}^{\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{2}^{n}}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{2}\in\left[{\left\lvert{(Q_{2})_{1}}\right\rvert}\right],\,\\
u,v\in\left[{\left\lvert{(\hat{T})_{1}}\right\rvert}\right]\\
t\in m\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{2}^{n}}\right)\end{subarray}},\,\end{split}

where
\left\{{K_{t}^{\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{2}^{n}}}\right\}_{t\in m\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{2}^{n}}
with

(315)

\displaystyle m\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{2}^{n}=\dim\left({\text{End}^{\,S_{n-1}\,}\left({\left({A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right)_{2}^{n}}\right)}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\autoref{prop:bounding_our_case}}}{{\leq}}\left({n+1}\right)^{\left\lvert{A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}}\right\rvert^{2}}

is the canonical basis to

Proposition E.5
.

For any

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}:=A_{2}Q_{2}\hat{T}
. The proof is based on a dual system consisting of

to

. Note, that

(341)

\displaystyle a^{*}_{p_{i}}a_{p_{j}}=\langle a_{p_{i}},a_{p_{j}}\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left\{{a_{p_{i}}}\right\}_{i}\text{ ONB}}}{{=}}\delta_{i,j}.\,

Consider the coordinate ring of homogenous polynomials of degree

on

denoted

. Importantly, as opposed to a free algebra generated from the same alphabet, in the coordinate ring, the ordering of letters in monomials is irrelevant.
We bijectively identify a representative element
(a,b)
of each orbit
O_{(a,b)}^{\mathcal{H}}
with a degree
n
monomial in the coordinate ring. Any other element
(c,d)\in O_{(a,b)}^{\mathcal{H}}
is mapped to the same monomial. Concretely, the map
15

15

Equivalently, we can define

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu\,:\,&W_{\mathcal{H}}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{n}\left({W_{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\\
&(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{n}a_{p_{i}}^{*}\end{split}

is an

(343)

\displaystyle\sigma\left({\mu\left({p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}\right)}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}a^{*}_{p_{\sigma^{-1}(i)}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}a^{*}_{p_{i}}=\mu\left({p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}}\right),\,

for any

.

. Concretely, by definition

(349)

\displaystyle g:=u_{\tau}\otimes u_{\gamma}=\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau^{\prime}\sim\tau,\\
\gamma^{\prime}\sim\gamma\\
\end{subarray}}\sum_{c,c^{\prime}\in C_{\lambda}}\text{sgn}(cc^{\prime})}_{\text{Young sym.}}\underbrace{\bigotimes_{y\in Y(\lambda)}F_{\tau^{\prime}\left({c(y)}\right),\gamma^{\prime}\left({c^{\prime}(y)}\right)}}_{n-\text{fold tensor product}},\,

where
F\in\left({\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{H}}^{*}}\right)^{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert\times\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}
is an encoding of the canonical basis of the dual space, i.e.
\left({F}\right)_{x,y}=a^{*}_{(x,y)}
. Then,

(350)

\displaystyle\sum_{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in\left({\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right]\times\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right]}\right)^{n}}g\left({\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}a_{p_{i}}}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}a^{*}_{p_{i}}\,\in\mathcal{O}_{n}\left({\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}}\right)

is a linear combination of
n
-degree dual monomials with coefficients

(351)

\displaystyle\begin{split}g\left({\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}a_{p_{i}}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref {eqn:g_def}}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau^{\prime}\sim\tau,\\
\gamma^{\prime}\sim\gamma\\
\end{subarray}}\sum_{c,c^{\prime}\in C_{\lambda}}\text{sgn}(cc^{\prime})\prod_{y\in Y(\lambda)}^{n}a^{*}_{\tau^{\prime}(c(y)),\gamma^{\prime}(c^{\prime}(y))}a_{p_{y}},\,\end{split}

where orthonormality between basis elements yields

(352)

\displaystyle\begin{split}a^{*}_{\tau^{\prime}(c(y)),\gamma(c^{\prime}(y))}a_{p_{y}}=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cc}1,&\text{if }\,p_{y}=\left({\tau^{\prime}(c(y)),\gamma^{\prime}(c^{\prime}(y))}\right),\\
0,&\text{else}.\end{array}\end{cases}\end{split}

Next, we show that the polynomial

(353)

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{m(\mathcal{H})}\left({u_{\tau}\otimes u_{\gamma}}\right)\left({C_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\mu(O_{r}^{\mathcal{H}})

with
n
-degree dual monomials representing each orbit can be equated to
G_{\tau,\gamma}(F)
from
Eq. 344
for a specific
F
. Concretely,

(354)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{r=1}^{m(\mathcal{H})}\left({u_{\tau}\otimes u_{\gamma}}\right)\left({C_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\mu(O_{r}^{\mathcal{H}})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref {eqn:g_def}}}{{=}}\sum_{r=1}^{m(\mathcal{H})}g\left({C_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}}\right)\mu(O_{r}^{\mathcal{H}})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref {eqn:adjacency_matrix}}}{{=}}\sum_{r=1}^{m(\mathcal{H})}g\left({\sum_{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in O_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}a_{p_{i}}}\right)\mu(O_{r}^{\mathcal{H}})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\text{ antilin. 2nd arg.}}}{{=}}\sum_{r=1}^{m(\mathcal{H})}\sum_{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in O_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}}g\left({\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}a_{p_{i}}}\right)\mu(O_{r}^{\mathcal{H}})\\
\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\begin{subarray}{c}\ref {eqn:mu_map},\,\ref {eqn:concrete_coeff},\,\ref {eqn:ONB_concrete}\\
\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right]\times\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right]^{n}\simeq\left\{{O_{r}{{}^{\mathcal{H}}}}\right\}_{r=1}^{m(\mathcal{H})}\end{subarray}}}{{=}}\sum_{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in\left({\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right]\times\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right]}\right)^{n}}g\left({\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}a_{p_{i}}}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{n}a^{*}_{p_{i}}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref {eqn:zeta}}}{{=}}\sum_{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in\left({\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right]\times\left[{\left\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\right\rvert}\right]}\right)^{n}}g\left({\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}a_{p_{i}}}\right)\zeta\left({\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}a^{*}_{p_{i}}}\right)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref {eqn:g_def}}}{{=}}\zeta\left({u_{\tau}\otimes u_{\gamma}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\zeta\text{ lin.}}}{{=}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau^{\prime}\sim\tau,\\
\gamma^{\prime}\sim\gamma\\
\end{subarray}}\sum_{c,c^{\prime}\in C_{\lambda}}\text{sgn}(cc^{\prime})\,\zeta\left({\bigotimes_{y\in Y(\lambda)}F_{\tau^{\prime}\left({c(y)}\right),\gamma^{\prime}\left({c^{\prime}(y)}\right)}}\right)\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau^{\prime}\sim\tau,\\
\gamma^{\prime}\sim\gamma\\
\end{subarray}}\sum_{c,c^{\prime}\in C_{\lambda}}\text{sgn}(cc^{\prime})\,\prod_{y\in Y(\lambda)}F_{\tau^{\prime}\left({c(y)}\right),\gamma^{\prime}\left({c^{\prime}(y)}\right)}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref {eqn:big_G_def}}}{{=}}G_{\tau,\gamma}\left({F}\right).\,\end{split}

In other words,
G_{\tau,\gamma}\left({F}\right)
is a linear combination of poly
(n)
-many (i.e.
m(\mathcal{H})
) monomials
\mu(O_{r}^{\mathcal{H}})
. Recall, any
Z\in\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
can be given in the canonical basis
\left\{{C_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}}\right\}_{r=1}^{m(\mathcal{H})}
to
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({\mathcal{H}}\right)
, i.e.

(355)

\displaystyle Z=\sum_{r=1}^{m(\mathcal{H})}z_{r}C_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}.\,

Since the
\left\{{z_{r}}\right\}_{r\in\left[{m(\mathcal{H})}\right]}
are a collection of class functions on orbits
\left\{{O_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}}\right\}_{r\in\left[{m(\mathcal{H})}\right]}
and the
\left\{{C_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}}\right\}_{r\in\left[{m(\mathcal{H})}\right]}
are linearly independent as basis elements, determining each
z_{r}
requires comparing
m(\mathcal{H})
many entries of
Z
with a corresponding element in each of the
C_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}
. Concretely, since the
C_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}
contain exclusively zeroes and ones, for
(x,y)\in O_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}
efficiently computed via
Eq. 301
we obtain
z_{r}=(Z)_{x,y}
. In summary, we have shown that given
\left\{{z_{r}}\right\}_{r\in\left[{m(\mathcal{H})}\right]}

(356)

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{m(\mathcal{H})}z_{r}u_{\tau}^{T}C_{r}^{\mathcal{H}}u_{\gamma}

can be computed efficiently.
∎

Appendix F
Representation theory

In this section, we primarily adopt a module-theoretic approach to representation theory. Nevertheless, group-theoretic language is occasionally employed where appropriate. The exposition is largely based on
[
FH04
,
Mel81
,
Eti+11
,
Las21
,
LF20
]
, and is intended as a review for the reader. We claim no novelty for the material presented.

We will briefly introduce the main mathematical objects relevant to this work. We assume familiarity with group theory. A ring
(R,+,\cdot)
is an abelian group under addition, a monoid (semigroup together with an identity) under multiplication and the multiplication is distributive with respect to the addition.
R
is unitary if it contains a multiplicative identity element. A division ring is a unitary ring in which the non-zero elements form a group under multiplication, i.e. to each such element there exists a multiplicative inverse. In a commutative ring, the multiplication is commutative. An associative algebra
\mathcal{A}
over a commutative ring
R
is a ring together with a ring homomorphism from
R
into
Z(\mathcal{A})
, the centre of
\mathcal{A}
.

Remark F.1
.

A field is a commutative division ring. By Wedderburn’s little theorem, any finite division ring is commutative and, therefore, a field.

Importantly,
\mathcal{A}
is also an
R
-module.

Definition F.2

(
R
-module)
.

Let
R
be a unitary ring with identity
1_{R}
. A left
R
-module, or a module over
R
is an additive abelian group
M
together with a left action
R\times M\rightarrow M
, described by
(\lambda,x)\mapsto\lambda x
, such that

(1)

\lambda(x+y)=\lambda x+\lambda y
,
\forall\lambda\in R,\,\forall x,\,y\in M
;

(2)

(\lambda+\mu)x=\lambda x+\mu x
,
\forall\lambda,\,\mu\in R,\,\forall x\in M
;

(3)

\lambda(\mu x)=(\lambda\mu)x
,
\forall\lambda,\,\mu\in R,\,\forall x\in M
;

(4)

1_{R}x=x
,
\forall x\in M
.

Remark F.3
.

A right
R
-module is defined analogously with a right action
M\times R\rightarrow M
described by
(x,\lambda)\mapsto x\lambda
. For
R
a commutative ring, the definitions coincide.

Remark F.4
.

Note that for a ring
R
, we refer to the set of
n\times n
matrices with entries from
R
, which form a ring under matrix addition and matrix multiplication named as the matrix ring
R^{n\times n}
or
M_{n}(R)
. Moreover, for
R
commutative,
R^{n\times n}
is an associative algebra.

A free
R
-module
M
is a
R
-module
M
with a basis, i.e. there exists a set
B\subseteq M
of linearly independent elements generating
M
.

Remark F.5
.

For any set
S
and ring
R
, the free
R
-module with basis
S
is the free module on
S
. Any vector space over a field is a free module by virtue of having a basis.

We will now relate these concepts to groups.

Definition F.6

(Group algebra)
.

For a finite group
G
and ring
R
, the group ring of
G
over
R
denoted
R\left[{G}\right]
is a free module on
G
, i.e. all formal
R
-linear combinations of elements from
G
. Furthermore, if
R
is a field,
R\left[{G}\right]
is a
R
-vector space with basis
G
, hence an
R
-algebra, which we refer to as the group algebra.

Remark F.7
.

By construction, the
R
-vector space structure naturally makes
R\left[{G}\right]
an abelian group under addition. Furthermore, the group multiplication is extended by
R
-bilinearity to yield the necessary multiplicative monoid structure. Moreover,
R\left[{G}\right]
inherits the identity from
G
, by definition of a free module the
R
-vector space dimension of
R\left[{G}\right]
is given by the order of
G
and
R\left[{G}\right]
is commutative if and only if
G
is abelian. Notably, the ring structure of
R\left[{G}\right]
allows us to apply general results on
R
-modules to
R\left[{G}\right]
-modules. We will mainly work with

(357)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]=\left\{{\sum_{g\in G}\alpha_{g}g\,:\,\alpha_{g}\in\mathbb{C}}\right\}.\,

Furthermore, we can think of the group algebra as complex valued functions on
G
, i.e.

(358)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]\,:\,G\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\,

as
G
forms a linearly independent basis of
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
.

The coefficients
\alpha_{g}
to an element of
R\left[{G}\right]
are class functions, i.e. constant on conjugacy classes of
G
.

There exists a natural connection between group representations and group algebra modules. Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field of characteristic zero.

Proposition F.8
.

Any
\mathbb{K}
-representation
\rho\,:\,G\rightarrow\text{GL}\left({V}\right)
of
G
yields a
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module structure on
V
,

(359)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cdot\,:\,\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]\times V&\rightarrow V\\
\left({\sum_{g\in G}c_{g}g,v}\right)&\mapsto\left({\sum_{g\in G}c_{g}g}\right)\cdot v:=\sum_{g\in G}c_{g}\rho(g)v.\,\end{split}

Conversly, every
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module
(V,+,\cdot)
defines a
K
-representation

(360)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho_{V}\,:\,G&\rightarrow\text{GL}\left({V}\right)\\
g&\mapsto\rho_{V}(g)\,:\,V\rightarrow V,v\mapsto\rho_{V}(g)(v):=g\cdot v\end{split}

of
G
.

Remark F.9
.

Note that
\text{Aut}(V)\subseteq\text{End}\left({V}\right)
denotes the subgroup of invertible endomorphisms, i.e. automorphisms on
V
. Note that
\text{GL}\left({V}\right)\simeq\text{Aut}(V)
and
\text{GL}\left({V}\right)\simeq\text{GL}_{n}(V)
as groups if
\dim(V)=n
.

Remark F.10
.

Note that every unitary ring
R
is an
R
-module where the
R\times R\rightarrow R
action is the multiplication in
R
and similarly, any field
\mathbb{K}
is a
\mathbb{K}
-vector space. A representation of a group
G
over a field
\mathbb{K}
is a
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module via
G
-action

(361)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cdot\;:\;G\times\mathbb{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{K}\\
(g,\lambda)\mapsto g\cdot\lambda:=\lambda\end{split}

extended by
\mathbb{K}
-linearity to
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
called the trivial
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module. Accordingly, we refer to
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
as a
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module as the regular
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module and denote it by
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]^{\circ}
.
G
and thus
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
act on
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
by conjugation extended by linearity.

Remark F.11
.

Since
G
is a group, the anti-automorphism

(362)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]&\rightarrow\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]\\
g&\mapsto g^{-1}\end{split}

naturally makes any left
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module into a right
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module.

Remark F.12
.

For
N,M
two
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-modules, the
\mathbb{K}
-balanced tensor product
N\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M
is also a
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module via diagonal action of
G
, i.e.

(363)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cdot\,:\,G\times(N\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M)&\rightarrow N\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M\\
(g,n\otimes m)&\mapsto g\cdot(n\otimes n):=gm\otimes gn\end{split}

extended to
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
via
\mathbb{K}
-linearity.

We are especially interested in substructures of
R
-modules.

Definition F.13
.

A submodule of an
R
-module
M
is a subgroup
N
of
M
that is stable under the action of
R
on
M
, in the sense that if
x\in N
and
\lambda\in R
then
\lambda x\in N
. Equivalently,
N
is a
R
-submodule of
M
, if and only if

(364)

\displaystyle\lambda x+\mu y\in N,\,\hskip 28.45274pt\forall x,y\in N,\forall\lambda,\mu\in R.\,

Accordingly, a non-empty subset
\mathcal{B}
of an
R
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}
s if

(365)

\displaystyle x-y\in\mathcal{B},xy\in\mathcal{B},\lambda x\in\mathcal{B},\,\hskip 28.45274pt\forall x,y\in\mathcal{B},\forall\lambda\in R.\,

Remark F.14
.

For any
R
-module
M
,
M
and
\left\{{0}\right\}
are always trivial submodules.

A module is simple if and only if it contains no proper nonzero submodules, while a semisimple module is decomposable into a direct sum of simple modules. We extend the notion to algebras and say that an algebra is semisimple if all its finite-dimensional modules are semisimple.

Remark F.15
.

Simple modules correspond to irreducible representations and semisimple to completely reducible representations. For finite groups and fields of characteristic zero, we can always assume there to be a semisimple decomposition.

Next, we introduce structure-preserving maps, i.e. homomorphisms between modules.

Definition F.16
.

If
M
and
N
are
R
-modules then a mapping
f\,:\,M\rightarrow N
is called an
R
-homomorphism if

(1)

f(x+y)=f(x)+f(y)
,
\forall x,y\in M
,

(2)

f(\lambda x)=\lambda f(x)
,
\forall x\in M
,
\forall\lambda\in R
.

M
and
N
are equivalent as
R
-modules, i.e.
R
-isomorphic, if there exists a bijetive
R
-homomorphism between them. The set of
R
-homomorphisms from
M
to
N
forms an abelian group under addition and is denoted by
\text{Hom}_{R}\left({M,N}\right)
. Furthermore, it admits the structure of an algebra. Moreover,
\text{End}_{R}\left({M}\right)
is the centralizer algebra of the
R
-action on
M
, i.e. endomorphisms on
M
commuting with the
R
-action.

Remark F.17
.

In representation theory,
G
-equivariant maps constitute an analogous concept to
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-homomorphisms.

While in general,
\text{Hom}_{R}\left({M,N}\right)
does not form an
R
-module restricting to
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
allows for the following proposition.

Proposition F.18
.

Let
M,N
be
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-modules. Then, the abelian group
\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left({M,N}\right)
is a
KG
-module via the cojugate
G
action

(366)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cdot\,:\,G\times\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left({M,N}\right)&\rightarrow\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left({M,N}\right)\\
(g,f)&\mapsto g\cdot f:M\rightarrow N,m\mapsto(g\cdot f)(m):=g\cdot f(g^{-1}\cdot m)\end{split}

extended to
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
by
\mathbb{K}
-linearity.

Remark F.19
.

Note, that
\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({M,N}\right)
is in general not a
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module.

Remark F.20
.

For a
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module
M
we can impose a
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module structure on its
\mathbb{K}
-dual
M^{*}:=\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left({M,\mathbb{K}}\right)
via

(367)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cdot\,:\,G\times M^{*}&\rightarrow M^{*}\\
(g,f)&\mapsto g\cdot f\,:\,M\rightarrow K,\,m\mapsto(g\cdot f)(m):=f(g^{-1}\cdot m)\end{split}

extended to
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
by
\mathbb{K}
-linearity. Furthermore, for another
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module
N
,
\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left({M,N}\right)=M^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}N
.

Importantly, Schur’s lemma characterizes
R
-homomorphisms.

Proposition F.21

(Schur
[
Mel81
]
)
.

Let
M
,
N
be simple modules over ring
R
. Then, any homomorphism
f:M\rightarrow N
of
R
-modules is either invertible or zero. Furthermore, the ring
\text{End}_{R}\left({M}\right)
of
R
-morphisms is a division ring.

Remark F.22
.

Since all division rings are simple,
\text{End}_{R}\left({M}\right)
is not decomposable.

We can further specify Schur’s lemma for the group algebra over an adequate field.

Proposition F.23

(Schur’s theorem for Group algebra
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
)
.

(1)

Let
V,W
be simple
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-modules. Then,

(a)

Any homomorphism of
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-modules
\phi\,:\,V\rightarrow V
is either zero or invertible. Equivalently,
\text{End}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V}\right)
is a division ring.

(b)

If
V\ncong W
, then
\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V,W}\right)=0
.

(2)

If
\mathbb{K}
is an algebraically closed field and
V
is a simple
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module, then

(368)

\displaystyle\text{End}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V}\right)=\left\{{\lambda\text{Id}_{V}\,:\,\lambda\in\mathbb{K}}\right\}\simeq\mathbb{K}.\,

Remark F.24
.

Significantly, by the fundamental theorem of algebra,
\mathbb{C}
is algebraically closed.

For a semisimple
R
-module, we collect the simple
R
-modules in
R
-isomorphic relation in a class denoted by

(369)

\displaystyle\text{Irr}\left({R}\right):=\left\{{\text{isomorphism classes of simple }R-\text{modules}}\right\}.\,

Definition F.25
.

For
M
a semisimple
R
-module and
S
a simple
R
-module, the
S
-homogeneous component of
M
, denoted
S(M)
, is the sum of all simple
R
-submodules of
M
isomorphic to
S
.

Proposition F.26

(Wedderburn / Wedderburn’s little theorem)
.

Let
R
be a semisimple ring, then the following assertions hold.

(1)

If
S\in\text{Irr}\left({R}\right)
, then
S\left({R^{\circ}}\right)\neq 0
. Furthermore,
\left\lvert{\text{Irr}\left({R}\right)}\right\rvert<\infty
.

(2)

We have

(370)

\displaystyle R^{\circ}=\bigoplus_{S\in\text{Irr}\left({R}\right)}S\left({R^{\circ}}\right),\,

where each homogeneous component
S\left({R^{\circ}}\right)
is a two-sided ideal of
R
and
S(R^{\circ})T(R^{\circ})=0
if
S\neq T\in\text{Irr}\left({R}\right)
.

(3)

Each
S\left({R^{\circ}}\right)
is a simple left Artinian ring, the identity element of which is an idempotent element of
R
lying in
Z(R)
.

The center to
R
decomposes into a direct sum of primitive central idempotents, each projecting onto an isotypical component of
R^{\circ}
. Characters encode how minimal central idempotents are constructed as linear combinations of group elements.

Remark F.27
.

Clearly, the image of
g\in End(V)
is in
\text{End}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V}\right)
if and only if
g\in Z\left({\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\right)
.

We will also be needing the Wedderburn-Artin theorem for algebras over a field.

Proposition F.28

(Corollary to Wedderburn’s and Artin-Wedderburn’s theorems)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a semisimple, split
16
16

16

In our considerations,
\mathbb{K}
is an algebraically closed field.

\mathbb{K}
-algebra and let
S\in\text{Irr}\left({\mathcal{A}}\right)
be a simple
\mathcal{A}
-module. Then,

(1)

For the regular (and semisimple)
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
the
S
-homogeneous component of
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
are isomorphic to a matrix algebra. Concretely,
S\left({\mathcal{A}^{\circ}}\right)\simeq\mathbb{K}^{n_{S}\times n_{s}}
with
\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\left({S\left({\mathcal{A}^{\circ}}\right)}\right)=n_{S}^{2}
;

(2)

\dim_{\mathbb{K}}(S)=n_{S}
;

(3)

\dim_{\mathbb{K}}=\sum_{S\in\text{Irr}\left({\mathcal{A}}\right)}\dim_{\mathbb{K}}(S)^{2}
;

(4)

\left\lvert{\text{Irr}\left({\mathcal{A}}\right)}\right\rvert=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\left({Z(\mathcal{A})}\right)
.

Importantly, we thus have the unique isotypic decomposition

(371)

\displaystyle R^{\circ}=\bigoplus_{S\in\text{Irr}\left({R}\right)}S\left({R^{\circ}}\right)\simeq\bigoplus_{S\in\text{Irr}\left({R}\right)}\mathbb{K}^{n_{S}\times n_{S}}.\,

into isotypical components
\left\{{S\left({R^{\circ}}\right)}\right\}_{S\in\text{Irr}\left({R}\right)}
.

Remark F.29
.

Notably, while this decomposition into isotypical components is unique, i.e. each component is unique, the decomposition of each isotypical component into isomorphic simple
R
-modules is not unique. Furthermore, other semisimple decompositions are basis dependent.

Importantly, an
R
-modules is simple if and only if it is a minimal left ideal. Minimal ideals are generated from minimal (or primitive) idempotents, i.e. those that cannot be decomposed into a sum of two nonzero, orthogoanl idempotents.

Proposition F.30

(
[
Mel81
]
, Theorem 6.13)
.

An endomorphism
f\in\text{End}_{R}\left({M}\right)
is an idempotent, if and only if it is a projection.

There is a strong connection between projections and submodules.

Proposition F.31

(
[
Mel81
]
, Theorem 6.14)
.

An
R
-module
M
is the direct sum of submodules
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
if and only if there are non-zero
R
-morphisms
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in\text{End}_{R}\left({M}\right)
such that

•

\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}=\mathcal{I}_{M},\,

•

(i\neq j)p_{i}\circ p_{j}=0.\,

Remark F.32
.

While at the moment, the existence of primitive idempotents to each simple R-module follows from its minimal ideal structure, in the case of
R=\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
, we will later use bijections to Young tableaux to construct these effectively.

Character theory allows us to formalize some aspect of the decomposition of
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
as a
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-module. Concretely, by Wedderburn’s little theorem, the homogeneous components are the images of idempotents from
Z\left({\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}\right)
. We can identify the space of class functions on
G
with
Z(\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right])
as vector spaces. The characters form a basis of the space of class functions on
G
.
Thus, we can construct elements from
Z(\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right])
via simple (irreducible) characters. The characters tell us how group elements need to be combined to yield idempotents as these must yield class functions when evaluated at group elements. Furthermore, each minimal central idempotent is determined by a simple character.

Proposition F.33
.

Let
V^{(i)}
be a simple
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-module in
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
and let
\chi^{(i)}
denote the corresponding character. Then,

(372)

\displaystyle e_{i}=\frac{d_{i}}{\left\lvert{G}\right\rvert}\sum_{g\in G}\chi^{(i)}\left({g^{-1}}\right)g

where
d_{i}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({V^{(i)}}\right)
is a central minimal idempotent onto the isotypical component to
V^{(i)}
.

Proposition F.34

(
[
Eti+11
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a finite-dimensional algebra. Then
\mathcal{A}
has finitely many simple modules
V_{i}
up to isomorphism. These simple modules are finite-dimensional. Moreover,
\mathcal{A}
is semisimple if and only if as an algebra,

(373)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\simeq\bigoplus_{i}\text{End}\left({V_{i}}\right)

where
V_{i}
are simple
\mathcal{A}
-modules.

Proposition F.35
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an algebra with simple
\mathcal{A}
-module
V
of finite dimension
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The space
\text{End}\left({V}\right)
with left
\mathcal{A}
action is a semisimple left
\mathcal{A}
-module such that

(374)

\displaystyle\text{End}\left({V}\right)\simeq V^{\oplus n}.\,

Proof.

Let
\left\{{v_{i}}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a basis of
V
, then

(375)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi\,:\,&\text{End}\left({V}\right)\rightarrow V^{\oplus n}\\
&x\mapsto(xv_{1},\ldots,xv_{n})\end{split}

is an isomorphism.
∎

Proposition F.36
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a finite-dimensional semisimple subalgebra of
\text{End}\left({V}\right)
for finite-dimensional vector space
V
. Then,

(376)

\displaystyle V\simeq\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}\otimes\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left({V_{i},V}\right)\simeq\bigoplus_{i}\left({V_{i}}\right)^{\oplus m_{i}}

with
\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left({V_{i},V}\right)
the multiplicity space of
V_{i}
in
V
of
\mathbb{C}
-vector space dimension
m_{i}
and for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
v\otimes f\in V_{i}\otimes\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left({V_{i},V}\right)
we have

(377)

\displaystyle a\left({v\otimes f}\right)=av\otimes f.\,

Proof.

By Schur’s lemma, we have the canonical identification of the semisimple finite-dimensional representation
V
of
\mathcal{A}
as
\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}\otimes\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left({V_{i},V}\right)
by considering the map

(378)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi\,:\,&\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}\otimes\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left({V_{i},V}\right)\rightarrow V\\
&v\otimes f\mapsto f(v)\end{split}

which is an isomorphism. The second isomorphism follows from
Prop. F.35
.
∎

Remark F.37
.

This semisimple decomposition is non-unique, as the choice of the
V_{i}
’s are up to isomorphisms. Concretely, while in the isotypic decomposition any isotypic component was a sum over all isomorphic simple modules, here we "choose" one simple module
V_{i}
take it to the
m_{i}
’s power. Clearly, there is freedom in the choice of
V_{i}
. However, the number of isomorphic simple modules in each isotypical component and the number of isotypical components is unique.

Remark F.38
.

For
V
the regular
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module,

(379)

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\left({S}\right)^{2}=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\left({\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({S,V}\right)}\right)=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\left({S(V)}\right)

Maschke’s theorem assures the semisimplicity of
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
as an algebra.

Proposition F.39

(Maschke)
.

Let
G
be a finite group and
\mathbb{K}
a field whose characteristic
17
17

17

We restrict to fields of characteristic
0
.
does not divide
\left\lvert{G}\right\rvert
. Then,
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
is a semisimple
\mathbb{K}
-algebra.

Remark F.40
.

Importantly, any
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module is semisimple. However, the concrete decomposition depends on the specific
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module under consideration. Moreover, not all simple
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module appear in all
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-modules. Furthermore, there might be multiple isomorphic copies of a simple
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module appearing.

We will now use Schur to translate the decomposition of semisimple
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module
V
to a decomposition of
\text{End}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V}\right)
.

Proposition F.41
.

Let
V
be a semisimple
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-module and
\left\{{V_{i}}\right\}_{i=1}^{t}
the corresponding simple
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-modules. Then,

(380)

\displaystyle\text{End}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V}\right)\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\mathbb{K}^{m_{i}\times m_{i}}

where
m_{i}=\dim_{\mathbb{K}}\left({\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V_{i},V}\right)}\right)
.

Proof.

Since
V
is semisimple, it admits a decomposition into simple
\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]
-modules

(381)

\displaystyle V\simeq\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}}.\,

Furthermore,
\text{End}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V_{i}}\right)\subseteq\text{End}\left({V_{i}}\right)
. By Schur’s lemma,
Hom
-space additivity and Wedderburn-Artin,

(382)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{End}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V}\right)&\simeq\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}V_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}},V}\right)\\
&=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}},V}\right)\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}},V_{j}^{\oplus m_{j}}}\right)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\text{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}},V_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}}}\right)\\
&=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\text{End}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V_{i}^{\oplus m_{i}}}\right)\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}M_{m_{i}}\left({\text{End}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V_{i}}\right)}\right)\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}M_{m_{i}}\left({\mathbb{K}}\right)\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\mathbb{K}^{m_{i}\times m_{i}}.\,\end{split}

∎

Proposition F.42
.

We have

(383)

\displaystyle\dim\left({\text{End}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V}\right)}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{t}m_{i}^{2}.\,

Proof.

Since
\text{End}_{\mathbb{K}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V}\right)\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\mathbb{K}^{m_{i}\times m_{i}}
and
\dim\left({\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\mathbb{K}^{m_{i}\times m_{i}}}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\dim\left({\mathbb{K}^{m_{i}\times m_{i}}}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{t}m_{i}^{2}
.
∎

From now on, let
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
.

Proposition F.43

(
[
FH04
]
)
.

Let
G
be a finite group and
V
a finite-dimensional right
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-module. If
U
is a simple left
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-module, then
V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}U
defined as the
\mathbb{C}
vector space

(384)

\displaystyle\left({V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}U}\right)/\left\langle vg\otimes u-v\otimes gu\,:\,v\in V,u\in U,g\in\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]\right\rangle

is a simple left
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V}\right)
-module.

We are interested in extracting from
V
a subset of elements such that under
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-action we recover all of
V
. Ultimately, we will reduce any problem formulated in terms of
V
to be formulated in terms of this representative set. In other words, due to linearity we are ultimately interested in the set of representative elements from a
G
-orbit partitioning of a basis to
V
. The decomposition of
V
given in
Prop. F.36
into simple
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-modules yields such a representative set. Concretely, for

(385)

\displaystyle V=\bigoplus_{i=1}\bigoplus_{j=1}V_{i,j}

with
V_{i,j}\simeq_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}V_{i,j^{\prime}}
for all
i,j,j^{\prime}
and
V_{i,j}
not isomorphic to
V_{k,j}
for
i\neq k
, choose
u_{i,j}\in V_{i,j}
, then

(386)

\displaystyle\left\{{\left({u_{i,1},\ldots,u_{i,m_{i}}}\right)\,:\,i\in\left[{k}\right]}\right\}

is a representative set. Importantly, the following porposition establishes when a given subset from
V
corresponds to a representative set.

Proposition F.44

(Representative set,
[
Pol20
]
Proposition 2.4.3)
.

Let
G
be a finite group with
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-module
V
. Let
k,m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
and
u_{i,j}\in V
for
i\in\left[{k}\right]
,
j\in\left[{m_{i}}\right]
. Then the set

(387)

\displaystyle\left\{{\left({u_{i,1},\ldots,u_{i,m_{i}}}\right)\,:\,i\in\left[{k}\right]}\right\}

is a representative for the action of
G
on
V
if and only if:

(1)

V=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]\cdot u_{i,j}
,

(2)

\forall i\in\left[{k}\right]
and
j,j^{\prime}\in\left[{m_{i}}\right]
, there exists a
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-isomorphism

(388)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi\,:\,\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]\cdot u_{i,j}&\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]\cdot u_{i,j^{\prime}},\,\\
&u_{i,j}\mapsto u_{i,j^{\prime}},\,\end{split}

(3)

\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i}^{2}\geq dim\left({\text{End}_{G}\left({V}\right)}\right)
.

The notion of the representative set together with a
G
-invariant inner product
18
18

18

Can be constructed for any
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-module, via

\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{G}
on
V
, i.e.

(389)

\displaystyle\left\langle gv,gw\right\rangle_{G}:=\left\langle v,w\right\rangle_{G}\hskip 28.45274pt\forall g\in G,v,w\in V

allows us to make the isomorphism in
Prop. F.41
concrete.

Remark F.45
.

While introducing such a
G
-invariant inner product at first seems arbitrary, our proposed isomorphism needs to yield the desired block structure. Concretely,
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-isotypical components are orthogonal w.r.t.
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{G}
. While the isotypical components of
V
are orthogonal w.r.t. a
G
-invariant inner product, the isomorphic simple modules within each isotypical component need not be. However, we can always find a decomposition of each isotypical component into isomorphic simple
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-modules, which are pairwise orthogonal w.r.t.
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{G}
.

Proposition F.46

(
[
Pol20
,
Gij09
]
, Proposition 2.4.4.)
.

Let
G
be a finite group,
V
a
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-module with
\left\{{V_{i}}\right\}_{i=1}^{t}
non
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-isomorphic simple
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-modules and
m_{i}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\text{Hom}_{G}\left({V_{i},V}\right)}\right)
for all
i\in\left[{t}\right]
. The map

(390)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi\,:\,&\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}\left({V}\right)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\mathbb{C}^{m_{i}\times m_{i}}\\
&A\mapsto\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\left({\left\langle Au_{i,j^{\prime}},u_{i,j}\right\rangle_{G}}\right)_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{m_{i}}\end{split}

is a bijection preserving positive-semidefiniteness.

Remark F.47
.

Importantly, checking each
m_{i}\times m_{i}
- matrix
\left({\left\langle Au_{i,j^{\prime}},u_{i,j}\right\rangle_{G}}\right)_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{m_{i}}
for positive semidefiniteness can be substantially simpler in terms of complexity, or in practice, in terms of computational cost, than checking
A
itself.

Furthermore, for
V\simeq\mathbb{C}^{Z}
for some finite set
Z
or some
Z\in\mathbb{N}
a
G
-invariant product is given by
\left\langle v,w\right\rangle:=w^{{}^{\dagger}}x
where
(\cdot)^{*}
is the conjugate transpose operation.

Remark F.48
.

For a finite group
G
on
\mathbb{C}^{Z}
, any representation

(391)

\displaystyle\begin{split}R\,:\,&G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{Z}\\
&g\mapsto R_{g},\,\end{split}

is unitary
[
Eti+11
, Theorem 3.11]
and thus,

(392)

\displaystyle g\cdot\left\langle v,w\right\rangle=\left\langle R_{g}v,R_{g}w\right\rangle=\left({R_{g}v}\right)^{\dagger}R_{g}w=w^{\dagger}R_{g}^{\dagger}R_{g}v=w^{\dagger}v=\left\langle v,w\right\rangle

proves the
G
-invariance of the inner product.

Thus, restructuring the representative sets to isotypical components as column vectors in a
Z\times m_{i}
-matrix, i.e. for all
i\in\left[{t}\right]

(393)

\displaystyle U_{i}=\left[{u_{i,1},\ldots,u_{i,m_{i}}}\right]\,\in\,\mathbb{C}^{Z\times m_{i}}

allows us to rewrite
Eq. 390
with the specified inner product. By
[
Pol20
, Definition 2.4.5]
the collection of all such
U_{i}
to each isotypical component in
\mathbb{C}^{Z}
is a representative set.

Proposition F.49

(
[
Pol20
]
)
.

The map

(394)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi\,:\,\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}\left({\mathbb{C}^{Z}}\right)&\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\mathbb{C}^{m_{i}\times m_{i}}\\
A&\mapsto\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}U_{i}^{*}AU_{i}\end{split}

is a bijection preserving positive semidefiniteness.

Remark F.50
.

Note that

(395)

\displaystyle\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}\left({\mathbb{C}^{Z}}\right)\simeq\left\{{A\in\mathbb{C}^{Z\times Z}\,:\,AR_{g}=R_{g}A,\,\forall g\in G}\right\}

Remark F.51
.

For
V\simeq\mathbb{R}^{Z}
,
U_{i}^{*}=U_{i}^{T}
.

As a direct consequence we have the following proposition.

Proposition F.52
.

Let
G
be a finite group,
V
a finite-dimensional right
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-module and
U
a simple left
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-module

(396)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi\,:\,&\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}\left({U}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}\\
&A\mapsto\left({\left\langle Au_{i,j^{\prime}},u_{i,j}\right\rangle_{G}}\right)_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{m},\,\end{split}

or for
V\simeq\mathbb{C}^{Z}
for a set
Z
of finite cardinality

(397)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi\,:\,&\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}\left({U}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}\\
&A\mapsto U_{i}^{\dagger}AU_{i},\,\end{split}

with
m=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\text{Hom}_{G}\left({U,V}\right)}\right)
.

Importantly, for the
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]}\left({V^{\otimes n}}\right)
decomposition, we will exploit Schur-Weyl duality as a consequence of the double centralizer theorem to obtain concrete information on the isomorphism.

Proposition F.53

(Double centralizer theorem)
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional vector space,
\mathcal{A}
a semisimple subalgebra of
\text{End}\left({V}\right)
, and
\mathcal{B}:=\text{End}_{\mathcal{A}}\left({V}\right)
the centralizer of
\mathcal{A}
action on
V
in
\text{End}\left({V}\right)
. Then,

(i)

\mathcal{B}
is semisimple,

(ii)

\mathcal{A}=\text{End}_{\mathcal{B}}\left({V}\right)
,

(iii)

As a module of
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
, we have

(398)

\displaystyle V\simeq\bigoplus_{i}U_{i}\otimes W_{i}

where
U_{i}
are all simple
\mathcal{A}
-modules and
W_{i}:=\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}\left({U_{i},V}\right)
are all simple
\mathcal{B}
-modules.

Proposition F.54

(
[
FH04
]
, Lemma 6.22)
.

Let
U
be a finite-dimensional right
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-module.

(i)

For any
c\in\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
, the canonical map
U\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]\cdot c
is an isomorphism of left
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}\left({U}\right)
-modules.

(ii)

If
W=\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]\cdot c
is a simple left
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-module, then
U\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}W
is a simple left
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}\left({U}\right)
-module.

(iii)

If
W_{i}=\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]\cdot c_{i}
are the distinct simple left
\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]
-modules, with
m_{i}
the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space dimension of
W_{i}
, then

(399)

\displaystyle U\simeq\bigoplus_{i}\left({U\otimes_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}W_{i}}\right)^{\oplus m_{i}}\simeq\left({Uc_{i}}\right)^{\oplus m_{i}}

is the decomposition of
U
into simple left
\text{End}_{\mathbb{C}\left[{G}\right]}\left({U}\right)
-modules.

F.1.
Representation theory of
S_{n}

While for general groups it is quite difficult or in fact impossible to make the arguments from the section above more concrete, in case of the symmetric group the situation looks different. We briefly present the relevant representation theory for the symmetric group
S_{n}
. For more information see e.g.
[
FH04
,
Sag13
]
.

Notably, the simple
\mathbb{C}\left[{S_{n}}\right]
-modules are in a bijection to its conjugacy classes which in turn are indexed in partitions.

Definition F.55
.

For
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
, a partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k})
of
n
into
k
parts denoted
\lambda\vdash_{k}\,n
is an ordered collection
\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{k}<0
of natural numbers such that
\lambda_{1}+\ldots+\lambda_{k}=n
. We will also refer to
k
as the height of the partition.

To each partition
\lambda\vdash n
, we can identify a Young diagram or shape,

(400)

\displaystyle Y(\lambda):=\left\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}:1\leq j\leq k,1\leq i\leq\lambda_{j}\right\}

i.e. a collection of boxes arranged in rows and columns according to the partition. Concretely, the Young shape of
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k})
, denoted
Y(\lambda)
has
\lambda_{j}
boxes in the
j
-th row for
j\in\left[{k}\right]
. A Young tableau is a Young shape with a numbering of the boxes by integers
1\,\ldots,n
. We refer to a canonical labelling of a Young diagram if the boxes are indexed from top left to bottom right, e.g. for partition
(3,2,2,1)
